Open editable notebook Download .ipynb

Specify the surroundings before rating a resource

A warm stream contains energy relative to a chosen reference. The amount that could become useful work depends on the surroundings available to receive heat. If stream and environment reach mutual thermal and mechanical equilibrium, there is no further physical work potential associated with their difference. This equilibrium is the physical dead state at \(T_0,p_0\). Exclude chemical reactions here and consider a steady flow with constant composition.

Imagine a reversible device that takes a stream from its current state to the dead state while interacting thermally only with the environment. The steady first law relates delivered work to changes in stream enthalpy, kinetic and potential energy, and heat. The reversible entropy balance gives heat received from the environment \(q_0=T_0(s_0-s)\). Eliminating that heat gives the specific flow exergy

\[\psi=(h-h_0)-T_0(s-s_0)+V^2/2+g(z-z_0).\]

Use consistent units for the kinetic and potential terms. The entropy contribution reduces the fraction of enthalpy that a reversible device can turn into useful work. At the dead state, with the same velocity and elevation as the reference, that work potential vanishes.

Cool a liquid to the environment

Take a stream of liquid water at approximately ambient pressure, 360 K, with negligible motion and elevation change. It flows at 0.20 kg/s towards a large environment at 300 K. Assume \(c_p=4.18\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\) constant. Integration gives

\[h-h_0=c_p(T-T_0),\]
\[s-s_0=c_p\ln(T/T_0).\]

The stream’s specific physical exergy is therefore

\[4.18[60-300\ln(360/300)]=22.2\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}.\]

Its enthalpy difference is much larger, 250.8 kJ/kg.

Cooling all the way to 300 K makes at most \(0.20(22.2)=4.44\,\mathrm{kW}\) of useful work available, even though the stream releases 50.16 kW of heat. Direct heat exchange with the environment delivers no shaft work. The environment entropy gain is \(50.16/300=0.1672\,\mathrm{kW/K}\), while the stream loses \(0.20(4.18)\ln(360/300)=0.1524\,\mathrm{kW/K}\). The difference, about 0.0148 kW/K, is the entropy production rate. Multiplication by \(T_0\) gives the lost 4.44 kW of work potential.

Include heat crossing another temperature boundary

Heat \(Q\) supplied reversibly by a reservoir at a uniform boundary temperature \(T_b\) carries exergy \(Q(1-T_0/T_b)\). This follows by comparing its energy with the environmental heat required to discharge the associated entropy \(Q/T_b\). A heat input of 10 kW from a 600 K reservoir in a 300 K environment carries 5 kW of exergy. A transfer from a finite cooling stream has changing source temperature and requires integration, as in the water example. Irreversibility in the device destroys exergy at rate \(T_0\dot S_{\rm gen}\); directing heat to a useful secondary process can retain part of the exergy that direct cooling would destroy.

Include the work potential of pressure

For an ideal gas already at \(T_0\), an elevated pressure still gives physical flow exergy \(RT_0\ln(p/p_0)\). Air at 300 K and twice environmental pressure has about \(0.287(300)\ln2=59.7\,\mathrm{kJ/kg}\) of pressure-related work potential although its ideal-gas enthalpy equals that at the dead state.

Derive the least work needed to heat a finite stream

Heat pumps are commonly compared by heat delivered per unit of electrical work. A second-law comparison additionally accounts for the temperature at which heat is delivered. Consider a steady stream heated from \(T_i\) to \(T_o\) at nearly environmental pressure, with constant heat capacity rate \(C=\dot m c_p\). A reversible heat pump extracts heat from surroundings at \(T_0\) and delivers it through a temperature-matched sequence of infinitesimal condensers. For one increment \(dQ=C\,dT\) at stream temperature \(T\), the reversed Carnot relation gives

\[dW_{\min}=dQ(1-T_0/T).\]

Integrating over the stream yields

\[W_{\min}=C[(T_o-T_i)-T_0\ln(T_o/T_i)].\]

This is the increase of its physical flow exergy, and is smaller than evaluating the Carnot work factor at the final high temperature for the whole heat duty. A device that condenses at a single temperature above the warmest outlet generates entropy during delivery to the colder portions. The result assumes that the source of heat is the environment at \(T_0\); a colder source would introduce an additional resource requirement.

For steady equipment receiving only electricity and environmental heat, with negligible parasitic flows, a meaningful second-law efficiency is the stream’s exergy increase divided by actual electrical input. Electric power is entirely work potential, whereas a kilowatt of heat near room temperature carries only a small fraction of a kilowatt of exergy.

Compare a heat pump with a resistance heater

Water flows at 0.10 kg/s from 293 K to 333 K in a 293 K environment. With \(c_p=4.18\,\mathrm{kJ/(kg\,K)}\), heat duty is \(0.418(40)=16.72\,\mathrm{kW}\). The exergy increase is \(0.418[40-293\ln(333/293)]=1.05\,\mathrm{kW}\) approximately. This relatively small figure reflects the modest average temperature lift. A well-insulated heat pump drawing 4.20 kW from the electrical supply has heating COP \(16.72/4.20=3.98\) and exergy efficiency about 25%. Its surroundings provide 12.52 kW of heat under the stated energy balance.

A resistance heater supplying the identical water service draws 16.72 kW when its losses to the room are negligible. It is nearly 100% efficient on the simple electrical-energy-to-heat basis, yet its exergy efficiency is only about 6.3%. For the heat pump the implied destruction of work potential is \(4.20-1.05=3.15\,\mathrm{kW}\); for resistance heating it is about 15.67 kW. Electricity used by a circulating pump, external heat leaks or a source below environmental temperature belongs in the appropriate device boundary before drawing a practical comparison. The heat-duty equality ensures that the machines are judged against the same delivered temperature service.

For the resistance case the entropy balance can be checked without using the exergy formula. Electrical work carries no entropy across the control surface, and assume no heat leakage. The stream increases its entropy at \(0.418\ln(333/293)=0.0535\,\mathrm{kW/K}\). Multiplying by the 293 K environment gives 15.7 kW destroyed, consistent with the difference between input work and stream exergy gain. This agreement depends on keeping the heater and heated stream in the same chosen boundary.

Why a real gas cools or warms across a throttle

Engineering thermodynamics and heat transfer

A gas passes through an insulated porous plug. Its pressure drops and its measured temperature changes. Start with the energy flowing through the plug and obtain a prediction from the equation of state.

Establish which property travels through the plug

A steady control volume with one inlet, one outlet and no shaft work has \(h_1+u_1^2/2+gz_1=h_2+u_2^2/2+gz_2\) when the casing is insulated. Choose the measurement stations in large, level pipes where changes in mean kinetic energy are negligible. Then \(h_1=h_2\). The pressure gradient is sustained by irreversible friction in the plug. Along the family of equilibrium outlet states, a small pressure change produces \(dT=\mu_{JT}dp\), with \(\mu_{JT}=(\partial T/\partial p)_h\). This family lets us compute endpoints without resolving the interior pore flow.

Recover the pressure dependence of enthalpy

Use molar properties. For a simple compressible pure substance, \(dh=T\,ds+v\,dp\). View entropy as a function of \(T,p\). At fixed pressure its temperature derivative is \(c_p/T\). To obtain its pressure derivative, differentiate \(g=h-Ts\). Its differential is \(dg=-s\,dT+v\,dp\). Equality of mixed derivatives gives

\[(\partial s/\partial p)_T=-(\partial v/\partial T)_p.\]

Substitution yields

\[dh=c_p\,dT+\left[v-T\left({\partial v\over\partial T}\right)_p\right]dp,\]
\[\mu_{JT}={T(\partial v/\partial T)_p-v\over c_p}.\]

For an ideal gas \(v=RT/p\) the two numerator terms cancel at all temperatures. Real gas attractions and finite molecular volume affect this near cancellation. Since \(dp<0\) for the pressure drop, positive \(\mu_{JT}\) indicates cooling. Check units by dividing cubic metres per mole by joules per mole per kelvin. The result is kelvins per pascal.

An instructive gas with an inversion temperature

Represent a dilute model gas by \(v=RT/p+B(T)\), with \(B=b-a/(RT)\). Here \(a,b\) are constants and the omitted equation-of-state terms become important at higher density. The ideal part cancels identically. Differentiating the correction gives

\[\mu_{JT}\simeq {T B'(T)-B(T)\over c_p}={2a/(RT)-b\over c_p},\]
\[T_{\rm inv}={2a\over Rb}.\]

With \(b=4.0\times10^{-5}\) m³/mol, \(a=0.080\) Pa m⁶/mol², \(R=8.314\) J/(mol K) and \(c_p=30\) J/(mol K), the inversion lies near 481 K. At 300 K, \(\mu_{JT}=0.0805\) K/bar. A modest drop from 3 bar to 1 bar cools this gas by about 0.161 K. At 600 K the derivative is about −0.0264 K/bar. The same pressure drop then warms the gas by 0.0528 K. Treat this as a local calculation. Over a large temperature range one should integrate the property derivative using a validated equation of state.

Alter the pressure drop while holding the attractive constant fixed. Locate the zero crossing at two pressure drops. Explain why it remains at the same temperature in this first-order calculation and why the cooling magnitudes change.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · infer the attractive correction

A low-density gas at 320 K gives a positive slope of 0.060 K/bar in a throttle experiment. Assume the same \(b,c_p\) as above. Estimate a and the inversion temperature. How would an unaccounted heat leak from a warm room bias the inference if the gas cools?

Show the inference and sign check

Convert the slope to \(6.0\times10^{-7}\) K/Pa. Rearranging gives \(a=(RT/2)(c_p\mu_{JT}+b)=0.0772\) Pa m⁶/mol², whence \(T_{\rm inv}=464\) K. A warm leak partially cancels cooling, making the observed outlet temperature drop too small. Because the pressure drop is also negative, the apparent positive slope is smaller. In this parameter fit the estimated a is therefore too low.

Further reading · MIT 2.43 · property relations and the Joule–Thomson coefficient

Finding the vapour fraction of an equilibrium flash

Engineering thermodynamics and heat transfer

A mixture enters a separator at given overall composition, pressure and temperature. Some components prefer the vapour more strongly than others. Derive a scalar equation for the fraction that evaporates and test for a two-phase solution before iterating.

Connect composition to the equilibrium ratios

Let \(F=L+V\) be the total molar feed and liquid and vapour flow rates. Overall component fractions are \(z_i\), liquid fractions \(x_i\) and vapour fractions \(y_i\). At equilibrium the chemical potential of each species agrees across phases. In an ideal gas over an ideal liquid this reduces to Raoult's law \(y_i p=x_i p_i^{\rm sat}(T)\), so \(K_i=y_i/x_i=p_i^{\rm sat}(T)/p\). A larger K enriches that species in the vapour. For a real mixture K can depend on phase composition and require an outer property iteration. Here use fixed positive K values to isolate the material balance.

Eliminate the component flows

Define \(\beta=V/F\). Component conservation gives

\[z_i=(1-\beta)x_i+\beta y_i=x_i[1+\beta(K_i-1)].\]

Thus \(x_i=z_i/[1+\beta(K_i-1)]\). For both compositions to sum to one, enforce \(\sum_i(y_i-x_i)=0\), giving the Rachford–Rice equation:

\[R(\beta)=\sum_i{z_i(K_i-1)\over1+\beta(K_i-1)}=0,\]
\[R'(\beta)=-\sum_i{z_i(K_i-1)^2\over[1+\beta(K_i-1)]^2}.\]

The denominators are positive for \(0\le\beta\le1\). With at least one non-unity K of positive feed fraction, R decreases strictly, so at most one physical root exists. A positive R(0) and a negative R(1) bracket two phases. If \(R(0)\le0\) the stable fixed-K split is all liquid; if \(R(1)\ge0\) it is all vapour, with equality corresponding to incipient phase appearance. When every K is one this balance cannot specify \(\beta\). A bracketed numerical method preserves the meaningful interval even near a phase boundary.

Perform a binary flash and change the feed composition

Choose \(K_A=2,\ K_B=0.5,\ z_A=z_B=0.5\). Then

\[R(0)=0.25,\ R(1)=-0.25.\]

Multiplying by the two denominators gives \(0.25-0.50\beta=0\), hence \(\beta=0.50\). Liquid fractions are \(x_A=1/3,\ x_B=2/3\), and vapour fractions are

\[y_A=2/3,\ y_B=1/3.\]

For 10 mol/s feed each outlet carries 5 mol/s. The A balance reads \(5(1/3)+5(2/3)=5\) mol/s.

Increase \(z_A\) to 0.60 at the same T, p and K. The physical root becomes \(\beta=0.80\). The phase compositions remain at the same two endpoints; their proportions change to accommodate the new feed. Indeed the overall A fraction is

\[0.20(1/3)+0.80(2/3)=0.60.\]

At \(z_A=0.75\) the feed lies beyond the vapour endpoint. Then R(1) is positive, and searching outside the interval would produce a vapour fraction above unity. Report a single vapour phase instead. An adiabatic flash at unknown temperature would require a further enthalpy balance.

Start from equal feed fractions and increase the fraction of A until the root leaves the physical interval. Predict which pure-phase endpoint is reached. Compare residual signs at both endpoints before interpreting the reported phase.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · determine whether an iteration is warranted

At an operating point take \(K_A=3,\ K_B=0.6\). Compute the liquid and vapour A fractions when both phases coexist. For \(z_A=0.30\), calculate \(\beta\). Repeat the phase test for \(z_A=0.10\) and explain why normalized hypothetical phase compositions can hide a failed balance.

Solve phase closure and the lever rule

Vapour closure gives \(3x_A+0.6(1-x_A)=1\), hence \(x_A=1/6\), \(y_A=1/2\). For overall fraction 0.30,

\[\beta=(0.30-1/6)/(1/2-1/6)=0.40.\]

At \(z_A=0.10\),

\[R(0)=0.10(2)+0.90(-0.4)=-0.16.\]

The feed remains liquid. Renormalizing an unphysical trial vapour composition could make it sum to unity while violating the original component and equilibrium equations. Check the physical endpoint signs first.

Further reading · Penn State, PNG 520 · elementary vapour–liquid equilibrium, flash objective function and numerical solution

When does a polar crystal change its preferred state?

Fields and the response of matter

Derive stable and unstable dielectric responses from a free-energy landscape. Separate a reversible small-signal capacitance from a switching experiment.

Why one voltage can support several measured polarisations

Some insulating crystals develop a spontaneous electric polarisation as temperature is lowered. The high-temperature phase may be symmetric under inversion so that states with polarisation \(+P\) and \(-P\) are equivalent in zero field. Near a continuous transition, treat one component of polarisation as an order parameter. A local free-energy density compatible with the symmetry is \(f(P)=a(T-T_c)P^2/2+bP^4/4\) with \(a,b>0\). The omitted terms limit how far in temperature or field this truncated expansion is reliable. The positive quartic term keeps the energy bounded when the quadratic coefficient is negative.

For a device whose applied electric field \(E\) is controlled, the appropriate potential in this simple model is \(g(P;E)=f(P)-EP\). The linear term records external work; changing what is held fixed changes the energy to minimise. Differentiation gives the constitutive equation \(E=a(T-T_c)P+bP^3\). An algebraic root is an equilibrium candidate. A stable local response also requires positive second derivative \(a(T-T_c)+3bP^2\). A very accurate rootfinder can return an unstable stationary point if this second test is omitted.

Find the equilibrium branches and their response

Introduce scaled polarisation \(p\), field \(e\) and temperature parameter \(s\) such that \(g=p^4/4+sp^2/2-ep\). Then \(e=p^3+sp\). For \(s>0\), the slope \(3p^2+s\) stays positive. Each field has one stable solution. At \(e=0\), \(p=0\) and the small-signal susceptibility \(dp/de=1/s\) increases when approaching the ideal critical point from above.

For \(s=-1,\ e=0\), stationary values are \(-1,0,+1\). The middle root has curvature −1 and is a local maximum. Each outer root has curvature 2 and is a minimum. The susceptibility about a selected outer state is \(1/2\) in the scaled units. The fact that the two domains can carry opposite zero-field polarisation does not make the harmonic response negative. The mere existence of a negative-slope segment in the cubic constitutive curve is insufficient to infer a stable isolated small-signal device with that differential response.

Derive the limit of a metastable homogeneous state

Imagine preparing the negative-polarisation state at \(s=-1\), then increasing the applied field slowly. A positive field lowers the energy of the positive state. The negative state can persist locally behind a barrier even after it ceases to be the global minimum. A model of perfectly homogeneous evolution without early nucleation follows this metastable branch until the curvature vanishes. Solve \(3p^2-1=0\), choose \(p=-1/\sqrt3\), and insert it into \(e=p^3-p\). The positive limiting field is

\[e=2/(3\sqrt3)\simeq0.3849.\]

At the opposite spinodal the signs reverse.

This is a limit of the particular energy model. Real switching can occur earlier through domain walls, nucleation, defects or thermally activated barrier crossing. Observing a smaller coercive field in a specimen is not evidence that the cubic equation was solved inaccurately. To compare an actual hysteresis loop with a calculation, state the sweep rate, boundary circuit, sample preparation and a dynamical or nucleation model in addition to equilibrium free energy.

Read a landscape rather than tracing an unlabelled loop

Open the live experiment

Set \(s=-1\). At zero applied field, locate the two equal wells. Increase positive \(e\) gradually and watch the negative well become shallower. The browser locates real stationary points of the cubic, classifies them by curvature and reports their positions. Its plotted potential shows the energy barrier separating basins. Choose \(s>0\) and repeat to see why fitting the same S-shaped hysteresis loop above a continuous transition would be inappropriate.

A below-transition sample can also form spatially varying domains. Adding a positive gradient term \(K(\nabla P)^2/2\) penalises interfaces, while depolarising fields can favour a mixture with reduced net surface charge. This competition sets new length scales absent from the homogeneous model. An electrode measuring total charge can return near-zero mean polarisation from oppositely oriented domains even though each domain is strongly polarised. A local probe and a bulk capacitance measurement therefore constrain different parts of the model.

Turn a capacitance measurement into a consistency test

For a small harmonic voltage imposed about a prepared local minimum, the differential response is \(\delta P/\delta E=1/[a(T-T_c)+3bP^2]\), provided the driving frequency is slow enough for this local variable to respond and the amplitude is small enough to stay in the same well. Add the vacuum and fast polarisation terms when converting to total displacement \(D\). In a parallel-plate geometry capacitance per area then follows from \(\delta D/\delta E\) divided by thickness.

A large-signal charge–voltage measurement crosses different branches and may dissipate work around a loop. The energy per volume over a closed polarisation cycle is \(\oint E\,dP\) with the sign convention for electrical work. A single equilibrium graph of \(f(P)\) returns to its initial state with no prescribed loop area. Measured hysteresis thus carries dynamical information that a polynomial fit to one reversible branch cannot uniquely reconstruct.

Tutorial

For \(s=-4\) in the scaled model, find zero-field minima and the positive homogeneous spinodal field.

Work through the solution

Zero-field minima lie at \(p=\pm2\). The negative spinodal is

\[p=-\sqrt{4/3}=-2/\sqrt3.\]

Substitution gives positive field \(16/(3\sqrt3)\simeq3.08\). The \( |s|^{3/2}\) dependence follows from the cubic.

A Newton solver initialised at \(p=0\) reports exact convergence for \(s=-1,e=0\). What additional observation rejects that result as the prepared stable state?

Work through the solution

Its residual is exactly zero but the curvature \(3p^2+s=-1\) is negative. A small perturbation lowers the potential. Check a measured weak-field slope or the relaxation after a perturbation, and compare the energy of the two minima.

Why can the homogeneous coercive field be much larger than the measured coercive field?

Work through the solution

The calculation removes a local barrier only when a spatially uniform state ceases to exist. A real material can switch by creating a small favourable domain and moving a wall; electrodes and defects can lower the needed activation energy. The relevant material and time scales differ.

Compare material descriptions

Use MIT's ferroelectrics and polarisation material to distinguish a microscopic source of displacement from the macroscopic free-energy model used here.

Moving constraints, boundary work and a changing energy

Analytical mechanics from constraints to invariants

A pendulum with a reeled string can gain energy while its support force does no virtual work. A bead on a spinning guide can accelerate without gravity. Follow the work done by the apparatus, derive the relation between a reduced energy and laboratory energy, and identify which quantity a measurement tests.

Parameterise the motion of a guide as well as the bead

Let a prescribed moving guide define positions \(\mathbf r(q,t)\). Its relative velocity along the guide is \(\mathbf r_q\dot q\) and its imposed local velocity is \(\mathbf r_t\), where \(t\) is differentiated at fixed coordinate. The kinetic energy \(T=m|\mathbf r_q\dot q+\mathbf r_t|^2/2\) includes a quadratic term in \(\dot q\), a mixed term linear in \(\dot q\), and a term independent of \(\dot q\). Dropping the last two merely because the guide motion is prescribed generally changes the bead equation. A term independent of \(\dot q\) may still depend on its position \(q\).

For \(L=T-V\) define \(p=\partial_{\dot q}L\) and \({\cal E}=p\dot q-L\). Differentiating along a solution of the Euler–Lagrange equation gives \(d{\cal E}/dt=-\partial_tL\) in the absence of extra nonconservative generalised forces. This identity involves the partial time derivative at fixed \(q,\dot q\). The laboratory energy is

\[E_{\rm lab}=m|\dot{\mathbf r}|^2/2+V.\]

Direct substitution gives \({\cal E}=E_{\rm lab}-m\dot{\mathbf r}\cdot\mathbf r_t\) for a single particle and ordinary position potential. Thus a time-independent reduced Lagrangian can conserve a quantity different from the laboratory mechanical energy. Which observer supplies a force, and which independent variables are held fixed, determine the energy being tracked.

Work supplied by a steadily rotating straight slot

A smooth horizontal guide turns about its inner end with prescribed angle \(\varphi=\Omega t\). Let \(r\) be the bead's distance from the pivot. The velocity is \(\dot r\hat r+r\Omega\hat\varphi\), and the reduced Lagrangian is \(m(\dot r^2+\Omega^2r^2)/2\). Its Euler–Lagrange equation is

\[\ddot r=\Omega^2r.\]

With \(r(0)=r_0,\dot r(0)=0\) the solution is \(r_0\cosh\Omega t\) until the bead leaves the slot. In the laboratory no force acts along the radial tangent of the slot, yet radial acceleration in polar coordinates is \(\ddot r-r\Omega^2\), precisely zero. The tangential acceleration \(2\Omega\dot r\) is provided by the slot wall.

The wall reaction \(R_\varphi=2m\Omega\dot r\) exerts a torque \(rR_\varphi\) on the bead. The driving motor balances the opposite reaction on the slot and supplies power \(\Omega rR_\varphi=2m\Omega^2r\dot r\). Differentiating \(E_{\rm lab}=m(\dot r^2+\Omega^2r^2)/2\) and using the radial equation gives the same power. The reduced energy \(m(\dot r^2-\Omega^2r^2)/2\) is constant. Its difference from \(E_{\rm lab}\) equals \(-\Omega L_z\), with \(L_z=mr^2\Omega\). This is a consequence of uniform imposed rotation, not a negative physical rotational kinetic energy.

For \(m=0.10\,{\rm kg}\), \(r_0=0.050\,{\rm m}\) and \(\Omega=4.0\,{\rm rad/s}\), consider \(t=0.25\,{\rm s}\). The radial position is \(77.2\,{\rm mm}\), radial speed \(0.235\,{\rm m/s}\), and wall reaction \(0.188\,{\rm N}\). The motor supplies about \(0.0580\,{\rm W}\) at that instant. Laboratory energy has risen from \(0.00200\,{\rm J}\) to approximately \(0.00752\,{\rm J}\). Measuring only the angular motor speed would miss the incoming mechanical work. The torque on the drive shaft or the changing moment of inertia supplies the other observable required for an energy budget.

A pendulum with prescribed length

Let a point pendulum have length \(l(t)>0\) and angle \(\theta\) from downward vertical. Using \(x=l\sin\theta\), \(y=-l\cos\theta\) gives \(T=m(\dot l^2+l^2\dot\theta^2)/2\) and \(V=-mgl\cos\theta\) for a fixed gravitational datum. The angular Euler–Lagrange equation becomes

\[\frac{d}{dt}(ml^2\dot\theta)=-mgl\sin\theta,\]
\[\ddot\theta+2\frac{\dot l}{l}\dot\theta+\frac{g}{l}\sin\theta=0 .\]

The term proportional to \(\dot l\dot\theta\) comes directly from differentiating angular momentum about the pivot. With increasing length the angular speed at fixed angular momentum decreases; reeling in has the opposite effect. It can look like damping or amplification in the angular equation even with no friction. A mechanical energy balance distinguishes the reversible exchange with a reel from energy lost as heat.

The radial Newton equation, taking outward as positive, is \(m(\ddot l-l\dot\theta^2)=mg\cos\theta-T_{\rm string}\). Hence \(T_{\rm string}=m(g\cos\theta+l\dot\theta^2-\ddot l)\). The tension power on the bob is \(-T_{\rm string}\dot l\). This has the correct sign when a heavy nearly vertical bob is lowered slowly: \(\dot l>0\) and the gravitational potential decreases as the string takes energy out. At the bottom of a swing the tension is larger, so a reel shortening the string there does more positive work per unit length than it would at a turning point. Timing a cyclic length change can pump an oscillation while the guide returns to precisely its initial length.

With \(m=0.50\,{\rm kg}\), \(l=1.0\,{\rm m}\), \(\dot\theta=2.0\,{\rm rad/s}\), and the bob at the bottom, a short interval of constant-rate shortening at \(\dot l=-0.030\,{\rm m/s}\) has tension \(6.91\,{\rm N}\) and delivers \(0.207\,{\rm W}\). When the bob is momentarily at rest at \(30^\circ\), the tension for the same length and zero radial acceleration is \(4.25\,{\rm N}\). Reversing the reel rate there would remove only \(0.127\,{\rm W}\). A complete finite cycle requires integrating the time-varying tensions and checking the acceleration during each reel reversal.

Translate a pivot and separate real forcing from a coordinate choice

For a pivot translating horizontally at prescribed \(X(t)\) and constant string length \(l\), write \(x=X+l\sin\theta\), \(y=-l\cos\theta\). The kinetic energy includes \(ml\dot X\dot\theta\cos\theta\). In the angular Euler–Lagrange equation the terms proportional to \(\dot X\dot\theta\sin\theta\) cancel, leaving \(l\ddot\theta=-g\sin\theta-\ddot X\cos\theta\). A constant pivot velocity therefore does not force the relative angle. Replacing the pivot velocity by its magnitude as a driving force would wrongly violate Galilean invariance. A horizontal pivot acceleration does force the angle; it appears as the opposite inertial acceleration in the translating frame.

For \(|\theta|\ll1\) and \(X=X_0\cos\omega t\), the equation becomes

\[\ddot\theta+\omega_0^2\theta=(X_0\omega^2/l)\cos\omega t,\]
\[\omega_0^2=g/l.\]

At drive frequencies well below \(\omega_0\),

\[\theta\simeq-\ddot X/g.\]

The bob nearly aligns with the effective gravity in the moving frame. Far above \(\omega_0\), the particular solution is approximately \(-X/l\); then the bob's absolute horizontal coordinate \(X+l\theta\) changes little even while the pivot moves. Near resonance the undamped steady particular amplitude is inappropriate, and the initial transient or a physical damping model matters.

Calculate work done by a moving wall in an elastic collision

A particle of mass \(m\) moves along a line and collides elastically with a wall whose prescribed laboratory velocity is \(U\) during the short contact. In the instantaneous wall frame the incoming relative velocity \(v^--U\) reverses, so

\[v^+=2U-v^-.\]

In the laboratory the impulse is \(J=m(v^+-v^-)=2m(U-v^-)\) and the kinetic energy change is \(\Delta K=m[(v^+)^2-(v^-)^2]/2=UJ\). The energy gained by the particle equals wall velocity times the impulse delivered by the wall. A perfectly elastic contact still changes laboratory kinetic energy when the wall moves, since elasticity was defined for the relative normal velocity.

Suppose a particle of mass \(0.20\,{\rm kg}\) moves right at \(3.0\,{\rm m/s}\) into a right wall moving left at \(0.50\,{\rm m/s}\). Its final velocity is \(-4.0\,{\rm m/s}\), and the impulse on the particle is \(-1.40\,{\rm N\,s}\). The particle's kinetic energy rises from \(0.90\) to \(1.60\,{\rm J}\), exactly \(UJ=0.70\,{\rm J}\). The actuator keeping the wall on schedule supplies the energy. When the same wall recedes to the right at \(0.50\,{\rm m/s}\), the outgoing velocity is \(-2.0\,{\rm m/s}\) and kinetic energy falls by \(0.50\,{\rm J}\). The difference is not symmetric under reversal of wall speed at fixed incoming particle velocity, because that reversal also changes the relative approach speed.

Now trap a particle between a fixed wall and a length \(l(t)\) varying slowly compared with the collision period. To leading order a right-wall collision changes the particle's speed by \(-2\dot l\), and such collisions occur about once in \(2l/v\). Hence \(dv/dt\simeq-(v/l)\dot l\), so \(vl\) is approximately constant. The one-dimensional action around a full bounce cycle is

\[J_{\rm act}=(2\pi)^{-1}(2mvl)=mvl/\pi.\]

This elementary wall argument explains the adiabatic action without requiring the smooth-force approximation at the instantaneous collision. If the wall speed varies quickly within a round trip, the collision phases matter and the averaged invariant can fail.

Starting with \(l_0=0.40\,{\rm m}\) and speed \(4.0\,{\rm m/s}\), slow compression to \(0.30\,{\rm m}\) predicts \(v\simeq5.33\,{\rm m/s}\). Its kinetic energy increases by a factor \(16/9\), supplied through the moving boundary. A pressure sensor at the wall measures impulse per time, \(2mv\) times the collision frequency \(v/(2l)\), giving average force \(mv^2/l\) at leading order. Multiplying this force by the inward speed gives the power required by the predicted energy law. An instantaneous compression while the particle is elsewhere would instead change no kinetic energy until a later collision. The final length alone does not determine the energy unless the rate protocol is specified.

Follow a string through a moving boundary in configuration and time

For a vertical moving pivot with height \(Y(t)\), coordinates \(x=l\sin\theta\), \(y=Y-l\cos\theta\) give kinetic energy \(m[\dot Y^2+2l\dot Y\dot\theta\sin\theta+l^2\dot\theta^2]/2\). Derive the angular equation by differentiating \(p_\theta=ml^2\dot\theta+ml\dot Y\sin\theta\). The terms containing \(\dot Y\dot\theta\cos\theta\) cancel as in horizontal translation; the remaining equation is

\[l\ddot\theta=-(g+\ddot Y)\sin\theta.\]

A vertical acceleration changes the restoring coefficient multiplicatively, unlike a horizontal displacement whose small-angle effect is an additive forcing term. The distinction is visible even when the two pivot motions have identical amplitudes and frequencies.

For \(Y=A\cos\Omega t\), the effective gravity oscillates as \(g-A\Omega^2\cos\Omega t\). The perfectly vertical solution remains an exact solution at any drive amplitude. A small deviation need not remain small, because its linear equation has a periodic coefficient. An observed growth of swing from a tiny initial disturbance near twice the natural frequency is a parametric effect. A camera viewing the pivot and bob together can separate relative angle from large imposed vertical displacement. Estimating angular amplitude from absolute bob height alone introduces a component at the drive frequency unrelated to the swing.

At modest drive \(A=1.0\,{\rm mm}\), \(\Omega=20\,{\rm rad/s}\), the pivot acceleration amplitude is \(0.40\,{\rm m/s^2}\), about four percent of gravity. A pendulum of length \(0.10\,{\rm m}\) has small-angle frequency \(9.90\,{\rm rad/s}\), putting this drive near twice its natural frequency despite the small displacement. For an exactly stationary vertical bob relative to the pivot, the string tension is \(m(g+\ddot Y)\) as long as that value is positive. A force measurement thus contains a direct drive-frequency signal even before transverse oscillation develops. Combining an angle sensor with a load cell helps distinguish contact loss from growth of parametric oscillation.

A fast vertical drive can also modify the slow stability of the inverted position. A quantitative treatment requires separating a fast small angular ripple from the slow tilt and averaging its feedback, with the support acceleration and frequency satisfying the relevant ordering. The instantaneous sign of \(g+\ddot Y\) alone is insufficient to establish the stability of the inverted solution. An upright motion that briefly samples a favourable sign each cycle may still drift away over many cycles. Compare initial perturbations at several drive phases and fit the envelope over complete periods before drawing a stability conclusion.

Treat the drive itself as a degree of freedom

A prescribed rotating slot conceals the energy source behind a fixed pattern speed. Replace the motor by a free rotating turntable with moment of inertia \(I_0\), and let its angle \(\varphi\) evolve dynamically. In the horizontal frictionless problem the total kinetic energy is \(m\dot r^2/2+(I_0+mr^2)\dot\varphi^2/2\). The angle is cyclic, so the total angular momentum \(P=(I_0+mr^2)\dot\varphi\) is constant. As the bead moves out, the turntable slows. The radial equation remains \(\ddot r=r\dot\varphi^2\), but its coefficient now decreases according to the total angular momentum instead of remaining externally fixed.

Eliminate \(\dot\varphi\) from the total energy to find

\[E=m\dot r^2/2+P^2/[2(I_0+mr^2)].\]

The reduced radial potential decreases outwards and feeds radial kinetic energy, but the total energy available is finite. Differentiating the potential gives a radial acceleration \(P^2r/(I_0+mr^2)^2\), consistent with the original radial equation. One should not simply insert \(\dot\varphi=P/(I_0+mr^2)\) into the original kinetic Lagrangian before varying \(r\); the correct fixed-momentum Routh reduction subtracts \(P\dot\varphi\), reversing the sign of the eliminated rotational term. The energy and Lagrangian methods agree once their controlled quantities are distinguished.

With \(I_0=0.020\,{\rm kg\,m^2}\), \(m=0.10\,{\rm kg}\), \(r_0=0.10\,{\rm m}\), initial angular speed \(4.0\,{\rm rad/s}\) and zero radial speed, total momentum is \(0.084\,{\rm kg\,m^2/s}\) and energy \(0.168\,{\rm J}\). At \(r=0.30\,{\rm m}\) the rotation rate has fallen to \(0.084/0.029=2.90\,{\rm rad/s}\). The remaining radial kinetic energy is \(0.168-0.084^2/(2\cdot0.029)=0.0463\,{\rm J}\), giving radial speed \(0.962\,{\rm m/s}\). Under a prescribed constant angular speed of four, the radial first integral instead gives \(\dot r=4\sqrt{r^2-r_0^2}=1.13\,{\rm m/s}\) there. The two runs started with identical positions and velocities but have different power connections.

The drive can also be modelled between those limits. Let a servo apply torque \(\tau_{\rm drive}=K_{\rm s}(\Omega_{\rm command}-\dot\varphi)\). Then \(d[(I_0+mr^2)\dot\varphi]/dt=\tau_{\rm drive}\), and the rate of increase of total mechanical energy is \(\tau_{\rm drive}\dot\varphi\). A high control gain with finite response time can approximate the prescribed-speed case over part of a trajectory, yet a rapid radial change can cause a measurable speed sag. Recording shaft torque and speed together tests the drive rather than attributing its lag to a fictitious radial friction on the bead.

Infer drive work from a closed loop of length and measured tension

The reel-pendulum experiment produces synchronised records of string force and length. Over a period in which the length returns to its original value, the work on the bob from the reel is \(-\oint T\,dl\). A graph of tension against length can enclose a signed area although the string force at each instant is perpendicular to the bob's allowed virtual angular displacement. That loop area measures energy transferred by the imposed control. If the bob returns to its full mechanical state and there are no other energy sources, any net positive reel work must go into losses. If only the string length returns, the bob's energy can increase cycle after cycle.

Suppose the reel shortens by \(20\,{\rm mm}\) during a high-tension interval averaging \(7.0\,{\rm N}\), then pays out the same length while the mean tension is \(4.5\,{\rm N}\). The net mechanical work on the bob is \(0.020(7.0-4.5)=0.050\,{\rm J}\) for that cycle. A torque sensor on a reel of changing winding radius must convert torque to tension with the actual instantaneous radius. A force signal delayed relative to the length encoder can create a spurious enclosed area, especially when tension changes rapidly near a turning point. Repeating the measurement slowly with a known hanging calibration weight provides a direct check of polarity and timing.

Derive rapid-pivot stabilisation rather than replacing gravity by its mean

The vertical-pivot equation can be written \(\ddot\theta+[g/l-(A\Omega^2/l)\cos\Omega t]\sin\theta=0\). The time average of the oscillatory coefficient is zero. Nevertheless a high-frequency pivot can alter slow stability because the small fast ripple in \(\theta\) correlates with the fast acceleration. Let \(\theta=\Theta+\xi\), where \(\Theta\) varies little in a drive period and \(\xi\) is small. Keeping the leading rapidly oscillating term gives \(\ddot\xi\simeq(A\Omega^2/l)\sin\Theta\cos\Omega t\), hence \(\xi\simeq-(A/l)\sin\Theta\cos\Omega t\). Its amplitude scales with displacement ratio \(A/l\), so large acceleration and small angular ripple can coexist.

Expand \(\sin(\Theta+\xi)\simeq\sin\Theta+\xi\cos\Theta\), insert the fast solution, and average. Terms linear in the cosine average to zero but the product of ripple and imposed acceleration gives a mean cosine square of one half. To leading order the slow equation is

\[\ddot\Theta+\frac{g}{l}\sin\Theta+\frac{A^2\Omega^2}{2l^2}\sin\Theta\cos\Theta=0.\]

It derives from an effective potential per angular inertia \(U_{\rm eff}/(ml^2)=-(g/l)\cos\Theta+[A^2\Omega^2/(4l^2)]\sin^2\Theta\), up to a constant. Linearising near \(\Theta=\pi+\eta\) gives

\[\ddot\eta+[A^2\Omega^2/(2l^2)-g/l]\eta=0.\]

The inverted state is stable within the averaged approximation if \(A^2\Omega^2>2gl\). The restoring contribution depends on squared pivot speed amplitude. Replacing effective gravity by its simple time average would miss it entirely.

Choose a rigid pendulum arm with \(l=0.20\,{\rm m}\), drive amplitude \(A=0.0040\,{\rm m}\), and \(\Omega=600\,{\rm s^{-1}}\). The small displacement ratio is \(0.020\), the pivot speed amplitude is \(2.4\,{\rm m/s}\), and \(A^2\Omega^2=5.76\,{\rm m^2/s^2}\), above \(2gl=3.92\,{\rm m^2/s^2}\). The predicted slow angular frequency about the inverted state is \(\sqrt{5.76/(2\cdot0.20^2)-9.81/0.20}=4.79\,{\rm s^{-1}}\), far below the drive frequency. A slow ripple of tilt \(0.10\,{\rm rad}\) has a leading drive-frequency micromotion of roughly \(0.0020\,{\rm rad}\). A camera sampling at an unfortunate phase can hide this micromotion or alias it into apparent slow drift.

The rigid arm in this example is essential. An ordinary string cannot push its bob when an averaged upright configuration requires alternating compression and tension throughout a fast cycle. Check the actual instantaneous radial reaction and the physical actuator's ability to deliver the large acceleration. Also check drive-period, arm elasticity and support resonance before identifying an observed stable wobble with the averaged rigid-arm prediction. The leading formula assumes small ripple and a clear separation between \(\Omega\) and all slow angular frequencies; near threshold the slow restoring coefficient is a small difference and higher-order effects or friction become relatively important.

Separate support work from energy in a moving coordinate

Prescribe an accelerating support. Compare the relative response with what a laboratory observer sees and identify how the drive supplies energy.

Open this experiment online

Distinguish two ways of moving an endpoint in Hamilton's principle

When a constraint itself is explicitly time dependent, the identity \(\delta g=\nabla g\cdot\delta x=0\) describes a virtual change at fixed time on the moving surface. A variational problem may instead allow the endpoint time to change. If a terminal point moves along a prescribed spacetime curve \(q_b(t)\), the endpoint variation includes \(dq_b=\dot q_b\,dt\); it is not an arbitrary tangent displacement at fixed laboratory clock. The boundary term in the on-shell action is \(p\,dq-H\,dt\). Reading these two terms separately tells which physical terminal conditions a derivation has assumed.

For a free particle of mass \(m\) moving in one dimension from the origin at time zero to \(q\) at time \(T\), the extremal action is \(S=m q^2/(2T)\). Its derivatives are \(S_q=mq/T=p\) and \(S_T=-mq^2/(2T^2)=-H\). If the terminal marker moves as \(q_b(T)=uT\), the action of the connecting extremal is \(mu^2T/2\). Its derivative along that endpoint line is \(p u-H=mu^2/2\), exactly the direct derivative. Holding the endpoint position fixed while differentiating instead gives \(-mu^2/2\). Both derivative experiments are legitimate, but the different signs demonstrate why one must state what is fixed.

Can a numerical action fit identify momentum and energy from a single sequence of terminal markers all moving at speed \(u\)?

Work through the solution

Along that one curve it returns the combination \(pu-H\). Separating momentum and energy requires independent endpoint variations in position and time, or another dynamical relation. For the free-particle model the known quadratic Hamiltonian supplies such a relation. For an unknown effective system a single endpoint track can leave different combinations indistinguishable. A grid of nearby fixed-time endpoints estimates \(p\), and a separate grid of terminal times at fixed place estimates \(-H\). This measurement interpretation is the finite-dimensional counterpart of separating virtual reaction work from actual boundary power.

Infer whether a moving wall may be treated as prescribed

In an actual impact apparatus the wall has finite effective mass \(M\). For a one-dimensional elastic impact with an initially moving wall, conservation of total momentum and reversal of relative velocity give

\[v^+=[(m-M)v^-+2MU]/(m+M),\]
\[U^+=[2mv^-+(M-m)U]/(m+M).\]

Only the limit \(M/m\to\infty\), or active feedback able to enforce the schedule on the collision time scale, gives the prescribed-wall expression used earlier. A motor that restores its target speed a second later may still behave as a finite inertia during the collision itself.

Take \(m=0.20\,{\rm kg}\), \(M=2.0\,{\rm kg}\), \(v^-=3.0\,{\rm m/s}\) and \(U=-0.50\,{\rm m/s}\). The finite wall sends the particle back at approximately \(-3.36\,{\rm m/s}\) and recoils to \(+0.136\,{\rm m/s}\), compared with the prescribed-wall prediction of \(-4.0\,{\rm m/s}\). Check that the initial and final total momenta both equal about \(-0.40\,{\rm kg\,m/s}\). Measuring wall speed immediately before and after contact separates an imperfectly enforced boundary from an inelastic impact; both can reduce the magnitude of particle rebound relative to an ideal prescribed boundary.

Tutorial on a prescribed moving apparatus

The rotating slot is held at fixed \(\Omega\), but the bead is initially given inward speed \(-\Omega r_0\). Predict its subsequent motion and whether the motor delivers or receives power.

Work through the solution

The general solution is a combination of growing and decaying exponentials. These initial conditions select \(r=r_0e^{-\Omega t}\). The wall reaction \(2m\Omega\dot r\) is negative, and its power \(2m\Omega^2r\dot r\) is also negative. The motor receives energy from the bead while maintaining its speed. The same geometric constraint can therefore inject or absorb energy depending on the actual trajectory. The origin is approached only asymptotically in the ideal solution.

At \(l=0.80\,{\rm m}\) and \(\theta=0\), a bob has zero angular speed and the prescribed reel tries to accelerate the length outward at \(12\,{\rm m/s^2}\). Can a string enforce this motion?

Work through the solution

The radial-force equation gives \(T=m(g-12)\), a negative tension. A string cannot push the bob outward. The reel pays out slack and the bob initially accelerates under gravity, so treating \(l(t)\) as its exact radius becomes inadmissible. A rigid telescopic arm capable of compression would impose a different physical constraint and could enforce the same geometrical schedule.

A pivot is displaced sinusoidally by \(2.0\,{\rm mm}\) at \(1.0\,{\rm Hz}\) with \(l=0.25\,{\rm m}\). Is the low-frequency tilt formula suitable?

Work through the solution

The natural angular frequency is \(\sqrt{9.81/0.25}=6.26\,{\rm rad/s}\), almost equal to the drive angular frequency \(2\pi=6.28\,{\rm rad/s}\). The low-frequency expansion requires a frequency much smaller than the natural one and fails here. Measure damping and initial conditions, or model a finite-time drive. A steady frictionless amplitude calculated from the small difference of squared frequencies is extremely sensitive to uncertainties in the length and drive frequency.

Compare a second rotating constraint and identify what the motor holds fixed.

David Tong's Lagrangian dynamics chapter develops explicitly time-dependent coordinate changes.

Vibrations of monoatomic and diatomic chains

Condensed matter and band theory

Derive normal modes from a local force law and identify the physical motion behind the acoustic and optical branches.

From first principles

Turn a local spring law into a phonon dispersion relation

Consider identical atoms of mass \(m\) spaced by \(a\) along a chain. Write \(u_j\) for the displacement from the equilibrium position of atom \(j\). For small changes of bond length, expand the bond energy to second order about its minimum. Its first derivative vanishes and its positive second derivative \(K\) is the measured bond stiffness. The bond on the right pulls with force \(K(u_{j+1}-u_j)\); the left bond contributes \(K(u_{j-1}-u_j)\). Newton’s law gives

\[m\ddot u_j=K(u_{j+1}-2u_j+u_{j-1}) .\]

Translation of a normal mode by one site can change its phase but not its shape. Insert \(u_j=Ue^{i(kja-\omega t)}\), divide by the common amplitude, and simplify:

\[-m\omega^2=K(e^{ika}+e^{-ika}-2)=-4K\sin^2(ka/2),\]
\[\omega=2\sqrt{K/m}\,|\sin(ka/2)|.\]

For wavelengths much larger than \(a\),

\[\sin(ka/2)\simeq ka/2.\]

The slope gives the sound speed \(a\sqrt{K/m}\). At \(k=0\) all atoms move together and no bond stretches, which explains the zero frequency. More than one atom per primitive cell permits internal relative motion even at \(k=0\), the ingredient needed for an optical branch.

Derivation and applications

Count the modes that crowd against a chain’s band edge

Why can a narrow frequency bin near the top of a vibration band contain a substantial share of all modes? Take a monoatomic chain of \(N\) equal masses \(m\) with one longitudinal displacement each. Impose periodic ends and use identical harmonic nearest-neighbour springs of stiffness \(K\) with spacing \(a\). The frequency on the positive half of the first zone rises monotonically to \(\omega_m=2\sqrt{K/m}\). Inverting that branch gives the wavevector bounding the states below a trial frequency,

\[k(\omega)=\frac{2}{a}\arcsin\!\left(\frac{\omega}{\omega_m}\right),\qquad 0\leq\omega\leq\omega_m .\]

Allowed wavevectors are spaced by \(2\pi/(Na)\). Counting the interval of width \(2k(\omega)\) includes both propagation directions. In a large chain the fractional count and its derivative per atom are therefore

\[F(\omega)=\frac{2k(\omega)}{2\pi/(Na)}\frac{1}{N}=\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin\!\left(\frac{\omega}{\omega_m}\right),\]
\[g(\omega)=\frac{dF}{d\omega}=\frac{2}{\pi\omega_m\sqrt{1-(\omega/\omega_m)^2}}.\]

Here \(g\,d\omega\) is a fraction of the modes. The normalization follows from \(F(\omega_m)=1\), as required by the count of independent displacements. Near the edge the slope of the dispersion tends to zero, so evenly spaced wavevectors map into progressively smaller frequency intervals. This causes an integrable singularity in the continuum density. A finite spectrometer bin always contains a finite count. A measured spectrum can also weight modes differently through a probe’s coupling to atomic motion.

Worked example

Count modes within the upper tenth of the band

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

For \(N=10{,}000\), the interval between \(0.9\omega_m\) and the upper edge contains \(N[1-(2/\pi)\arcsin(0.9)]\simeq2871\) modes, allowing a mode or two for endpoints. A uniform distribution in frequency would give 1000. A nearly stationary wave packet near the upper edge and a large number of modes per unit frequency have the same origin in the small group velocity.

Second application

Use the spectral median to infer a spring stiffness

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

An ideal chain with atomic mass \(5.00\times10^{-26}\,\mathrm{kg}\) has half of its longitudinal modes below an ordinary frequency of \(4.00\,\mathrm{THz}\). Setting \(F=1/2\) gives

\[\omega_{1/2}/\omega_m=\sin(\pi/4).\]

The upper frequency is \(5.66\,\mathrm{THz}\), and \(K=m(2\pi f_m)^2/4=15.8\,\mathrm{N/m}\). The frequency ratio is unchanged by conversion to angular frequency, but the final stiffness requires that conversion.

Try it · Chain count

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

From bond extension to a travelling displacement

Let atoms of mass \(m\) on a line be coupled by springs of stiffness \(K\), spaced \(a\) apart. The spring to the right pulls site \(n\) by \(K(u_{n+1}-u_n)\); the left contributes \(K(u_{n-1}-u_n)\). A uniform shift produces no force. Translation symmetry suggests \(u_n=u e^{i(kna-\omega t)}\). Substitution gives

\[\omega^2(k)=\frac{2K}{m}(1-\cos ka)=\frac{4K}{m}\sin^2(ka/2).\]

The sound speed at long wavelength is \(a\sqrt{K/m}\), consistent with a continuum rod whose one-dimensional stiffness is \(Ka\) and whose mass per length is \(m/a\). At \(k=\pi/a\) neighbouring atoms move in opposition and the slope of the dispersion vanishes. A wave packet made of nearby \(k\)'s moves with the group velocity \(d\omega/dk\), which can differ greatly from the phase velocity \(\omega/k\) near the boundary.

Background · Why must one vibration have exactly zero frequency?

Give every atom the same displacement. If the potential energy depends only on separations, no bond changes length and no atom experiences a restoring force. Differentiate the force with respect to this uniform translation. For one displacement direction the resulting row-sum condition on the force constants is

\[\sum_j K_{ij}=0,\]
\[D\,(\sqrt{m_1},\ldots,\sqrt{m_N})^T=0,\]
\[D=M^{-1/2}KM^{-1/2}.\]

With unequal masses the translational eigenvector is uniform in physical displacement, while its mass-weighted components vary. A nonzero frequency at zero wavevector may reveal missing neighbour interactions in a numerical model. A real spring to a substrate also breaks translation symmetry and produces a physical gap. The boundary conditions decide which explanation is plausible.

Introduce an internal coordinate

Place masses \(m,M\) alternately, with spacing \(a/2\) between neighbours and cell period \(a\). Let their displacements in cell \(n\) be \(Ue^{ikna}\) and \(Ve^{ikna}\) times the same time factor. The two force balances contain sums of neighbouring amplitudes. For example the A mass sees B in cell \(n\) and in cell \(n-1\), giving \(m\omega^2 U=2KU-K(1+e^{-ika})V\). Exchanging the masses and conjugating the phase gives the second row. Requiring a nonzero solution yields

\[mM\omega^4-2K(m+M)\omega^2+4K^2\sin^2(ka/2)=0 .\]

There are two nonnegative solutions for \(\omega^2\) per \(k\). With \(N\) cells under periodic boundary conditions this gives \(2N\) longitudinal modes, exactly matching the number of independent displacements. At \(k=0\) the lower branch has \(U=V\) and zero frequency. For the upper branch the centre of mass stays fixed, \(mU+MV=0\), with \(\omega^2=2K(1/m+1/M)\). An electric field can excite some optical modes in polar crystals; the branch name alone does not guarantee optical activity.

A gap and a sound speed answer different questions

At the zone boundary \(1+e^{ika}=0\), so the sublattices decouple. If \(M=4m\), the two edge frequencies are \(\sqrt{K/(2m)}\) and \(\sqrt{2K/m}\), a factor of two apart. When the masses become identical the two folded branches meet, recovering the original monoatomic chain in a doubled cell. An apparent branch boundary created by plotting in a smaller zone need not be a physical frequency gap.

Expand the determinant for small \(k\) on the lower branch. Since \(\omega^2\) scales as \(k^2\), discard the term of order \(k^4\). This gives

\[v_s=a\sqrt{K/[2(m+M)]}.\]

The two springs in a cell act in series for long waves, while both masses contribute to inertia. The sound speed alone determines a stiffness-to-mass ratio; measuring the upper branch supplies independent information about the internal motion.

Try it · Diatomic

Change the inputs and compare the recalculated curves with the preceding derivation and baseline figure. The axes give the units; the numerical readout states the model assumptions and any limiting cases. Reset the inputs to return to the reference setting.

Open this live calculation

Convert a mode count into thermal predictions

Quantisation assigns a harmonic mode levels \(\hbar\omega(j+1/2)\). The thermal part of its mean energy is \(\hbar\omega/[\exp(\hbar\omega/k_BT)-1]\). Low-temperature excitations thus select long-wavelength acoustic modes while high-frequency optical modes remain scarcely populated. Counting states in a three-dimensional sphere gives a low-frequency density proportional to \(\omega^2\) for linear branches. Rescaling the energy integral by \(\hbar\omega/k_BT\) gives thermal energy proportional to \(T^4\) and heat capacity proportional to \(T^3\). Dimensionality and linear dispersion are essential assumptions behind that power.

Count thermally generated vacancies

A mode calculation for a perfect crystal starts with one atom on every site. Heating a real sample can leave some atoms at a surface or reservoir and some lattice sites empty. How large should that defect population be even at thermal equilibrium? Consider \(N\) equivalent sites and \(n_v\) independent vacancies. At fixed temperature and pressure let \(g_f\) be the Gibbs cost of making one vacancy and transferring its atom to the reservoir. It may contain local vibrational contributions. We count the separate entropy of choosing the missing sites explicitly.

There are \(W=N!/[n_v!(N-n_v)!]\) such arrangements. With \(x=n_v/N\) and macroscopic occupations, Stirling’s formula gives

\[\frac{G-G_0}{N}=xg_f+k_BT\left[x\ln x+(1-x)\ln(1-x)\right].\]

Minimizing with respect to \(x\) gives

\[g_f+k_BT\ln[x/(1-x)]=0.\]

The second derivative is positive. Thus \(x=1/(1+e^{g_f/(k_BT)})\). For positive \(g_f\gg k_BT\) there are few vacancies and \(x\simeq e^{-g_f/(k_BT)}\). Each vacancy costs energy, but at tiny \(x\) the number of possible positions grows fast enough to favour a nonzero population. A finite microscopic specimen can still contain zero vacancies at a given instant.

Take \(g_f=0.800\,\mathrm{eV}\) as constant over the chosen range and \(k_B=8.6173\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{eV/K}\). At \(900\,\mathrm K\), \(k_BT=0.07756\,\mathrm{eV}\) and \(x=3.31\times10^{-5}\). A region with \(10^{15}\) sites contains an expected \(3.31\times10^{10}\) empty sites. At \(600\,\mathrm K\) the equilibrium fraction drops to \(1.91\times10^{-7}\). Rapid cooling may leave an excess because vacancies must migrate to sinks before the concentration relaxes. The formation free energy in this calculation sets an equilibrium population. A migration barrier would enter the time to attain it.

A light substitution can localize a vibration

An infinite monatomic chain has a continuous vibration band, while a single altered atom can support a vibration spatially concentrated near one site. Let identical springs of stiffness \(K\) connect masses \(m\) on a chain of spacing \(a\), except the central mass at site zero is \(M\). Seek a symmetric localized solution with alternating neighbouring amplitudes \(u_n=A(-q)^{|n|}e^{-i\omega t}\) for \(0<q<1\). Alternation is motivated by the top of the perfect-chain band where adjacent atoms oscillate out of phase. Exponential decay supplies the localization.

At any ordinary site away from the impurity, the equation \(-m\omega^2u_n=K(u_{n+1}+u_{n-1}-2u_n)\) gives

\[m\omega^2=K(2+q+q^{-1}).\]

Since \(q+q^{-1}>2\), this frequency lies above the perfect-chain band edge \(\omega_{\max}=2\sqrt{K/m}\). At site zero both neighbours have displacement \(-q u_0\), giving \(M\omega^2=2K(1+q)\). Eliminating \(\omega\) and cancelling \(1+q\) yields

\[q=\frac{M}{2m-M},\]
\[\omega^2=\frac Km(2+q+q^{-1}).\]

The physical condition \(0<q<1\) requires a lighter central mass \(0<M<m\). A heavy isotope can still scatter travelling waves or produce resonant response within the band. This ansatz predicts no exponentially localized symmetric mode above the band for the heavier substitution. Its absence follows from the admissible decay factor rather than an assumption that a heavy impurity cannot move.

Predict a localized spectral peak

Take host atomic mass \(m=4.00\times10^{-26}\) kg, spring stiffness \(K=40.0\) N/m, lattice spacing \(a=0.300\) nm and impurity \(M=m/2\). The host upper angular frequency is

\[2\sqrt{40.0/(4.00\times10^{-26})}=6.32\times10^{13}\,\mathrm{rad/s}.\]

For the impurity \(q=1/3\), so \(\omega^2=(16/3)K/m\), giving \(\omega=7.30\times10^{13}\) rad/s or \(f=11.6\) THz. The ordinary chain ends near \(10.1\) THz.

The neighbour amplitude is minus one third of the central amplitude, and the second neighbour has plus one ninth. Expressing the absolute envelope as \(e^{-|n|a/\xi}\) gives localization length \(\xi=a/\ln3=0.273\) nm. As \(M\) approaches \(m\) from below, \(q\) approaches one, localization length diverges and the extra frequency merges with the band edge. A finite-chain calculation should therefore vary chain length before claiming that a very weak impurity mode is spatially localized: a box shorter than its decay length cannot separate that broad tail from a standing host mode.

Spatially resolved displacement data can provide two independent estimates of impurity mass. Invert the decay-factor relation to obtain \(M/m=2q/(1+q)\) from neighbouring displacement amplitudes. Separately insert the measured localized frequency and the same host force constant in the central equation of motion. Agreement tests the assumption that the impurity changes only inertia. A substituted atom may also change the two neighbouring springs: in that case the observed frequency and decay factor can no longer be fitted by altering mass alone. This inversion distinguishes an isotope-like substitution from a stronger local bonding perturbation.

A weak second impurity far away perturbs the localized mode only through its exponentially small tail. Bringing two such impurities within a few decay lengths allows the modes to mix and split in frequency, offering another test of their spatial localization.

Filling electronic states and measuring the Fermi surface

Condensed matter and band theory

Use exclusion and state counting to predict carrier energies, thermal response and the effect of dimensionality.

From first principles

Count the states in a filled Fermi sphere

Take independent spin-one-half electrons in a large cube of volume \(V=L^3\) with periodic boundary conditions. The condition \(e^{ik_xL}=1\) permits \(k_x=2\pi n_x/L\), with the same spacing in the other directions. Thus each allowed wave vector occupies reciprocal volume \((2\pi)^3/V\). For the free-particle energy \(E=\hbar^2k^2/(2m)\), all states below energy \(E\) lie in a sphere. Counting its volume and two spin states gives

\[N(E)=2\frac{V}{(2\pi)^3}\frac{4\pi}{3}\left(\frac{2mE}{\hbar^2}\right)^{3/2}.\]

At zero temperature the exclusion principle fills these single-particle states up to \(E_F\). Writing \(n=N/V\), solve the counting formula at that upper limit:

\[k_F=(3\pi^2n)^{1/3},\]
\[E_F=\frac{\hbar^2}{2m}(3\pi^2 n)^{2/3}.\]

The number of available states in a thin energy shell is the derivative \(D(E)dE\). In three dimensions \(D(E)\propto\sqrt E\) because the shell area grows with \(k^2\) while \(dk/dE\) falls as \(1/k\). This counting assumes an isotropic parabolic band. Additional valleys or a different effective dimension change the result.

Background · Fermi level
\[f(E)=1/[1+\exp((E-\mu)/k_BT)]\]

At equilibrium a one-electron level at the chemical potential has mean occupancy one half per available state. Spin multiplicity enters the number of states separately.

Background · Density of states
\[g(E)=dN_{\rm states}/dE\]

State density and state occupation are independent factors. Their product, integrated over a band, gives the mean particle number.

Derivation and applications

Obtain the pressure of a filled Fermi sea by compressing its states

Even at zero temperature a box of mobile electrons exerts a pressure on its boundaries. Consider \(N\) noninteracting spin-one-half fermions in a three-dimensional volume \(V\), with fixed isotropic mass \(m\) and quadratic energy. Take the bottom of the band as a volume-independent energy reference. Every state below \(E_F\) is occupied once per allowed spin state. Since the density of states including spin has the form \(D(E)=C\sqrt E\), dividing two elementary integrals eliminates the normalization constant,

\[\frac{U}{N}=\frac{\int_0^{E_F} E\,C\sqrt E\,dE}{\int_0^{E_F} C\sqrt E\,dE}=\frac35 E_F .\]

State counting gives

\[E_F=(\hbar^2/2m)(3\pi^2 N/V)^{2/3}.\]

During a uniform compression at fixed \(N\) the upper occupied energy rises. At zero entropy the energy derivative with respect to box volume is the negative pressure, so

\[P=-\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial V}\right)_N=\frac{2U}{3V}=\frac25 nE_F,\]
\[n=\frac NV .\]

Density appears both explicitly and through \(E_F\). A second volume derivative gives the positive resistance to a small fractional compression,

\[B=-V\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial V}\right)_N=\frac53P=\frac23 nE_F .\]

Both \(P\) and the bulk modulus \(B\) have units of energy per volume. The factor \(5/3\) distinguishes the change of pressure from pressure itself. An actual solid also has electrostatic and lattice contributions. Extracting the electronic contribution from a measured solid modulus requires information about these other stresses and the volume dependence of the band parameters.

Worked example

Estimate the electronic stiffness of a simple metal

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

Take \(n=5.00\times10^{28}\,\mathrm{m^{-3}}\) and \(E_F=5.00\,\mathrm{eV}\). With \(1\,\mathrm{eV}=1.602\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm J\), the product \(nE_F\) is \(4.01\times10^{10}\,\mathrm{Pa}\). The pressure contribution is \(16.0\,\mathrm{GPa}\) and the modulus is \(26.7\,\mathrm{GPa}\). A one percent volume decrease would raise this pressure by approximately \(0.267\,\mathrm{GPa}\) in the linear response regime.

Second application

Predict a finite compression without linearizing

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

Suppose a separate trapped gas starts at pressure \(12.0\,\mathrm{kPa}\) and is compressed slowly to \(0.800\) of its original volume at fixed particle number and negligible entropy. Its pressure becomes

\[12.0(1/0.800)^{5/3}=17.4\,\mathrm{kPa}.\]

Its energy per particle rises by only \((1/0.800)^{2/3}=1.16\) times. The larger pressure ratio also contains the increase in particles per unit volume.

Try it · Fermi pressure

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Count occupied states at zero temperature

Consider a cubic box of volume \(V\) with periodic boundaries and free electrons of effective model mass \(m\). Allowed wavevectors occupy a volume \((2\pi)^3/V\) per state for each spin. At zero temperature each state below the chemical potential is filled. For \(\epsilon=\hbar^2 k^2/2m\) the filled region is a sphere and each \(k\) has two spin states. Hence

\[N=2\frac{V}{(2\pi)^3}\frac{4\pi k_F^3}{3},\]
\[k_F=(3\pi^2 n)^{1/3},\]
\[E_F=\frac{\hbar^2 k_F^2}{2m}.\]

With \(n=8.5\times10^{28}\) m⁻³ and the free electron mass, \(k_F\) is about \(1.36\times10^{10}\) m⁻¹ and \(E_F\) about 7.0 eV. The corresponding temperature \(E_F/k_B\) is roughly 81,000 K. Ordinary room temperature therefore changes occupation only near the outer surface of this sea in a simple metal. This is a model estimate; anisotropic bands and multiple pockets change its interpretation.

The density of states is a derivative

Replacing \(k_F\) by \(k(E)=\sqrt{2mE}/\hbar\) gives the integrated state count below energy \(E\). Its derivative is

\[D(E)=\frac{V}{2\pi^2}\left(\frac{2m}{\hbar^2}\right)^{3/2}\sqrt E,\]
\[D(E_F)=\frac{3N}{2E_F}.\]

Check dimensions by integrating \(D(E)\,dE\) to recover a count. In two dimensions the occupied area grows as \(k^2\) and the density per energy is constant for a parabolic band. In one dimension the count grows as \(k\) and the density has an integrable \(E^{-1/2}\) singularity at its bottom. A large density of states means many states fit in a narrow energy window. It does not imply a large group velocity: a flat dispersion may create the large density.

OneSimulation · What would a probe see at the last atom?

An open one-dimensional chain with one orbital per atom has standing-wave eigenvectors. Counting their energies gives a spectrum for the complete specimen. An ideal local probe couples to the amplitude on the atom below it. For a probe over site \(j\), broaden each spectral line with a normalised Gaussian \(g_\eta\) of experimental width \(\eta\).

\[\rho_j(E)=\sum_n |\psi_n(j)|^2 g_\eta(E-E_n),\]
\[\int \rho_j(E)\,dE=1.\]

The integral counts one orbital per spin at either site. Near a band edge the standing waves vary slowly and have a small amplitude next to the missing neighbour. Their large bulk density of states can coexist with a weak end signal. The OneSimulation eigensolve uses the same Hamiltonian for both curves. Only the projected site changes.

"""Local spectroscopy at an open chain end and in its interior."""
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from onesim.numerics.linear.matrices.dense_linear_algebra import symmetric_eigensystem

sites = 81
hopping = 1.0  # eV
broadening = 0.09  # eV, standard deviation of the energy resolution
hamiltonian = -hopping * (np.eye(sites, k=1) + np.eye(sites, k=-1))
levels, vectors = symmetric_eigensystem(hamiltonian)
energy = np.linspace(-2.6 * hopping, 2.6 * hopping, 1001)
kernel = np.exp(-0.5 * ((energy[:, None] - levels) / broadening)**2)
kernel /= np.sqrt(2 * np.pi) * broadening
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(6.6, 3.5), layout="constrained")
for site, label in [(0, "End site"), (sites // 2, "Centre site")]:
    weights = np.abs(vectors[site])**2
    density = kernel @ weights
    assert np.isclose(np.trapezoid(density, energy), 1, atol=1e-6)
    print(f"{label}: local density at zero energy = {density[len(energy)//2]:.3f} /eV")
    ax.plot(energy, density, label=label)
ax.set(xlabel="Energy (eV)", ylabel="Local density per site and spin (1/eV)")
ax.grid(alpha=0.2)
ax.legend()
fig.savefig("condensed-matter.png", dpi=180)

Why only a shell contributes to heat capacity

At finite temperature the occupation is \(f(E)=[1+e^{(E-\mu)/k_BT}]^{-1}\). Heating can promote an electron only into a vacant state. Deep in the filled sea all nearby destinations are occupied. The active shell has width of order \(k_BT\), involving about \(D(E_F)k_BT\) states; the typical energy change is another factor \(k_BT\). Thus the thermal energy increases quadratically and the heat capacity rises linearly with \(T\).

A coefficient follows by expanding the smooth density of states across that shell. At fixed particle number, the relevant excess energy to leading order is \(\int (E-E_F)D(E_F)[f(E)-\Theta(E_F-E)]\,dE\). The filled and empty sides contribute equally to this order. Substituting \(x=|E-E_F|/k_BT\) and using \(\int_0^\infty x/(e^x+1)\,dx=\pi^2/12\) gives

\[C_{\rm el}=(\pi^2/3)D(E_F)k_B^2T.\]

This expansion requires the density to be smooth on the thermal scale; a band edge at the chemical potential needs separate treatment.

Separate two low-temperature contributions

In a three-dimensional simple metal combine \(C_\mathrm{el}=\gamma T\) with the phonon result \(C_\mathrm{ph}=\beta T^3\). Plot \(C/T\) against \(T^2\); the intercept estimates \(\gamma\), and the slope estimates \(\beta\). If two points are \((4,3)\) and \((16,6)\) in K² and mJ mol⁻¹ K⁻², respectively, \(\beta=0.25\) mJ mol⁻¹ K⁻⁴ and \(\gamma=2.0\) mJ mol⁻¹ K⁻². Curvature across the fitted interval would be evidence against this two-term description. To go beyond a free sphere we next retain the periodic potential of the ions.

Count charges and electronic compressibility in two dimensions

A two-dimensional electron layer illustrates how changing available spatial dimensions alters the density of states and an electrical measurement. Assume one parabolic subband \(E=E_c+\hbar^2(k_x^2+k_y^2)/(2m^*)\), spin degeneracy two and no valley degeneracy. A square sample of area \(A\) with periodic boundary conditions supplies one state per spin per reciprocal area \((2\pi)^2/A\). Counting the disk up to \(k_F\) gives areal density \(n_s=k_F^2/(2\pi)\), hence

\[E_F-E_c=\frac{\pi\hbar^2n_s}{m^*},\]
\[D_A(E)=\frac{dn_s}{dE_F}=\frac{m^*}{\pi\hbar^2}\]

above the subband edge at zero temperature. Unlike the square-root density in three dimensions, each occupied parabolic two-dimensional subband contributes a constant step. When an additional subband begins to fill it supplies another step, possibly with different degeneracy or mass. Temperature and disorder broaden the step in a real measurement.

An electrostatic gate must both create a field across the insulating spacer and raise the layer’s chemical potential. For areal capacitance \(C_g'\) of the spacer and increment of sheet charge magnitude \(dQ'=e\,dn_s\), the required voltage is

\[dV=dQ'/C_g'+dE_F/e.\]

The second term defines the quantum capacitance \(C_q'=e^2D_A\). Their inverse capacitances add in series. For a small density of states the electronic contribution measurably lowers the total capacitance even when the geometric dimensions are known precisely.

Measure which capacitance limits a device

Take \(m^*=0.067m_e\) and \(n_s=2.00\times10^{15}\) m⁻². Then \(k_F=\sqrt{2\pi n_s}=1.12\times10^8\) m⁻¹ and \(E_F-E_c=7.15\) meV. At 4 K the thermal scale \(k_BT=0.345\) meV is smaller than this separation, supporting the degenerate estimate. The electronic capacitance per area is \(0.0448\) F/m². For a spacer with \(C_g'=0.0100\) F/m², the measured series value is \((1/0.0100+1/0.0448)^{-1}=0.00818\) F/m².

Over a gate area of \(100\) μm², the predicted capacitance is \(0.818\) pF rather than the purely geometric \(1.00\) pF. Treating that measured value as a change of dielectric thickness would misidentify an electronic effect. In the limit of a very high density of states, the quantum capacitance becomes large and the geometric value is recovered. Near depletion the assumptions of a sharp occupied subband and uniform layer require scrutiny. Comparing capacitance with an independent sheet-density measurement can test whether a low reading reflects depleted electronic states or simply a different insulating spacer.

Using the series result, a small gate swing can be split into two measurable energy changes. A 10.0 mV swing at the stated operating point changes sheet charge magnitude by \(8.18\times10^{-5}\) C/m², or sheet density by \(5.10\times10^{14}\) m⁻². The geometric voltage increment is \(8.18\) mV and the chemical-potential contribution is \(1.82\) mV, corresponding to a Fermi-level shift of 1.82 meV. Their sum recovers the imposed swing. This decomposition assumes the layer remains in the same subband and equilibrates through its electrical contact on the capacitance measurement timescale.

A slow measurement with significant leakage instead mixes capacitance with conductance and can corrupt both estimated voltage contributions.

Count states added by raising the Fermi level

For a parabolic band, states within a radius \(k\) fill a length, area or volume in reciprocal space. Since \(k\) scales as \(\sqrt E\), the integrated count varies as \(E^{d/2}\). Normalise at one energy to compare exponents independently of the sample dimensions.
