Varying height and independently calibrating the measurement noise are appropriate diagnostics before attributing a discrepancy to bead dimensions.

Tutorial · Test a force-law inference with power and concentration controls

At fixed bead radius a trap is operated at two powers differing by a factor of two. Measured corner frequencies increase from 42 to 84 Hz, but rms displacement falls only from 110 to 87 nm. What does the harmonic model with fixed drag and temperature predict? Estimate a power-independent additive position-noise variance that could reconcile the two readings.

Use two variances to separate scale and offset

The doubled corner gives doubled stiffness if drag is unchanged. Physical thermal variance should halve. The predicted rms at higher power would be 77.8 nm if the lower-power value were entirely physical. Let the low-power measured variance be \(v+b\) and the high-power one \(v/2+b\), where \(b\) is a constant additive noise variance. Solving from 12,100 and 7,569 square nanometres gives \(v=9062\) and \(b=3038\) square nanometres, or approximately 55.1 nm rms noise.

This is a large inferred detector noise and demands an independent immobilised reference check. Power-dependent heating of the liquid changes temperature and viscosity, so an unchanged product inferred from only two quantities cannot prove that heating is absent. Test several powers, reverse their order to reveal slow drift and vary bead concentration to exclude overlapping particles in the tracking window. The inferred additive term should remain constant across those controls if it comes from the specified detector model.

Tolić-Nørrelykke and colleagues · position and force calibration using thermal and driven measurements

How an unobserved environment changes a quantum state

Quantum states, dynamics and measurement

Model a qubit and a two-state environment by a joint unitary. Derive channels, the continuous-time master equation and the relation between longitudinal and transverse relaxation. A recorded event and an unread detector produce different conditional predictions.

Derive loss of coherence from a larger unitary system

A qubit prepared in \(\alpha|0\rangle+\beta|1\rangle\) interacts with an environment initially in \(|e\rangle\). A joint unitary correlates \(|0,e\rangle\to|0,e_0\rangle,\ |1,e\rangle\to|1,e_1\rangle\), with both environmental states normalised. The joint output is a pure state. Its qubit density matrix follows by tracing over a complete environment basis. Populations stay \(|\alpha|^2,|\beta|^2\). The off-diagonal entry is \(\alpha\beta^*\langle e_1|e_0\rangle\). Orthogonal environment records remove coherence from all local qubit observations; identical records leave its magnitude intact. Partial distinguishability gives a continuous range in between. Energy need not flow in this controlled dephasing model. An observed reduction in fringe contrast can arise from entanglement with unobserved degrees of freedom even when total evolution is unitary.

Consider instead amplitude loss from an excited qubit to an initially empty environment mode:

\[|0,0_e\rangle\mapsto |0,0_e\rangle,\qquad |1,0_e\rangle\mapsto\sqrt{1-p}|1,0_e\rangle+\sqrt p|0,1_e\rangle .\]

The two output states remain orthogonal and unit-normalised, so this map on the prepared environment subspace can be extended to a unitary on the full joint space. If environment state \(k\) is unobserved, sum the associated unnormalised alternatives in density-matrix form:

\[\rho'=\sum_kK_k\rho K_k^\dagger.\]

Here \(K_0=|0\rangle\langle0|+\sqrt{1-p}|1\rangle\langle1|\) and \(K_1=\sqrt p|0\rangle\langle1|\). Since \(\sum K_k^\dagger K_k=I\), trace is preserved. The excited population obeys \(\rho_{11}'=(1-p)\rho_{11}\) and the coherence \(\rho'_{01}=\sqrt{1-p}\rho_{01}\). Averaging state vectors before forming outer products would introduce interference between orthogonal environmental records that have already been traced out.

Take a controlled infinitesimal limit

In a memoryless approximation each small interval \(dt\) meets a fresh environment record with vanishing probability of a previously lost excitation returning. Let jump operators \(L_j\) have units \(1/\sqrt{\mathrm s}\), set \(K_j=\sqrt{dt}\,L_j\) for events and \(K_0=I-dt(iH/\hbar+\sum L_j^\dagger L_j/2)\). Expanding the completeness relation shows trace preservation through first order in \(dt\). Expand \(\sum K_k\rho K_k^\dagger-\rho\), divide by \(dt\) and keep the finite terms:

\[\frac{d\rho}{dt}=-\frac i\hbar[H,\rho]+\sum_j\left(L_j\rho L_j^\dagger-\tfrac12 L_j^\dagger L_j\rho-\tfrac12 \rho L_j^\dagger L_j\right).\]

The first term of each dissipative bracket adds probability after an event and the anticommutator subtracts probability from the no-event branch. Their traces cancel. This construction is completely positive, meaning that the same local channel remains positive even when tensored with identity on an unused entangled ancilla. Positivity only on isolated input states would be too weak: transposition maps density matrices to density matrices on a single system, yet partial transposition can produce a negative eigenvalue when applied to half an entangled pair. The Lindblad form embodies the relevant stronger constraint for the assumed Markovian dynamics.

Independent loss and pure dephasing can be represented by \(L_1=\sqrt{\Gamma_1}|0\rangle\langle1|\),

\[L_\phi=\sqrt{\Gamma_\phi/2}\,\sigma_z.\]

The factor one half in the dephasing operator fixes our convention for \(\Gamma_\phi\). Direct substitution gives \(\dot\rho_{11}=-\Gamma_1\rho_{11}\), while \(\dot\rho_{01}\) acquires \(-(\Gamma_1/2+\Gamma_\phi)\rho_{01}\) in addition to its Hamiltonian phase. Thus

\[T_1=\Gamma_1^{-1},\]
\[T_2^{-1}=(2T_1)^{-1}+\Gamma_\phi .\]

At zero temperature with these mechanisms, \(T_2\le2T_1\). This upper bound is tied to definitions and assumptions. A broad ensemble with static detuning spread, a driven nonequilibrium sample or a refocused pulse sequence may yield an observed envelope with a different shape and timescale. Fitting every contrast decay with one exponential and interpreting that number as the homogeneous \(T_2\) can assign an unphysical dephasing rate.

Update a state when an individual event is recorded

If an ideal detector reports branch \(j\), the probability is \(p_j=\operatorname{tr}K_j\rho K_j^\dagger\), and the normalised conditional output is \(K_j\rho K_j^\dagger/p_j\). The conditional evolution depends on which environment observable is measured; summing outcomes gives the same physical unconditioned channel. In the loss model, detecting one emission places the qubit in its ground state. Absence of an emission also carries information. At finite duration \(t\), no emission acts by \(K_0=|0\rangle\langle0|+e^{-\Gamma_1t/2}|1\rangle\langle1|\) for the simple undriven zero-temperature case.

From initial \((|0\rangle+|1\rangle)/\sqrt2\), at \(t=T_1\ln2\) the no-event probability is

\[(1+1/2)/2=3/4.\]

Conditional excited probability is

\[(1/2)/(1+1/2)=1/3.\]

With the event branch in the ground state, the unconditioned excited probability is \((3/4)(1/3)=1/4\), agreeing with exponential decay of its initial one half. Repeatedly renormalising only no-event trajectories and calling their mean the ensemble population would overestimate survival. If the event detector misses photons, “no click” groups genuine no-emission with undetected emissions and its conditional state is generally mixed.

Use heating and cooling to infer the reservoir

Allow upward and downward transitions at rates \(\Gamma_\uparrow,\Gamma_\downarrow\). Rate equations give

\[\dot p_1=\Gamma_\uparrow(1-p_1)-\Gamma_\downarrow p_1,\]
\[p_{1,\infty}=\frac{\Gamma_\uparrow}{\Gamma_\uparrow+\Gamma_\downarrow},\quad\]
\[p_1(t)=p_{1,\infty}+[p_1(0)-p_{1,\infty}]e^{-(\Gamma_\uparrow+\Gamma_\downarrow)t}.\]

Both upward and downward rates contribute half their sum to coherence decay. If the reservoir is in thermal equilibrium, detailed balance gives

\[\Gamma_\uparrow/\Gamma_\downarrow=e^{-\hbar\omega/(k_BT)}.\]

The population relaxation time measures their sum and equilibrium population measures their ratio, allowing a substantive check of a proposed bath temperature. The relation is a thermodynamic assumption in addition to the two-state rate model. A pumped bath can have the same measured relaxation time with an entirely different stationary population.

For a 5.00 GHz transition, \(\hbar\omega/k_B=h(5\,\mathrm{GHz})/k_B\simeq0.240\) K. A stationary excited fraction 0.10 would imply \(T=0.240/\ln9=0.109\) K under equilibrium. With population relaxation 20 μs the sum of rates is \(5.0\times10^4\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\), giving 5000 upward and \(45\,000\) downward transitions per second for the respective source populations. Verified independent upward and downward preparations test more than fitting the decay of an initially excited state alone.

Discriminate static detuning, Markovian dephasing and coherent error

A Ramsey experiment prepares an equal superposition, allows free evolution for time \(t\), then converts relative phase to a population by a final analysis pulse. If the detuning \(\delta\) is fixed in each run but varies randomly between runs, then the ensemble coherence is multiplied by the characteristic function \(C(t)=\int p(\delta)e^{-i\delta t}d\delta\). Here \(\delta\) is an angular frequency. For a Gaussian detuning of mean \(\delta_0\) and standard deviation \(\sigma\),

\[C=e^{-i\delta_0t}e^{-\sigma^2t^2/2}.\]

A uniform detuning offset shifts fringe phase without reducing the contrast of a fully coherent, identically repeated preparation. A Lorentzian distribution yields an exponential envelope which can resemble homogeneous Markovian dephasing in a simple time scan. The fitted functional form alone may therefore fail to identify the physical cause of lost contrast.

An ideal instantaneous \(\pi\) pulse about \(x\) halfway through free evolution reverses the effect of a static \(z\)-detuning on relative phase: \(e^{-i\delta t\sigma_z/4}\sigma_x e^{-i\delta t\sigma_z/4}=\sigma_x\), since \(\sigma_x\) anticommutes with \(\sigma_z\). All static offsets refocus at the echo. Independent white Markovian dephasing accumulated in each half is uncorrelated and cannot be undone in this way. The deterministic Hamiltonian error and a rapidly fluctuating environment can share a short Ramsey contrast while giving different echo contrasts. If the pulse itself has finite duration or an angle error, the algebraic cancellation is no longer exact; repeating at two pulse powers tests that particular assumption.

Take \(\sigma/2\pi=20.0\) kHz and negligible damping. At \(t=10.0\,\mu\mathrm s\) the free Ramsey contrast from Gaussian detuning alone is

\[e^{-(1.257)^2/2}=0.454.\]

An ideal echo returns unity. Add independent \(T_1=80\,\mu\mathrm s,\ T_\phi=50\,\mu\mathrm s\). The homogeneous coherence time is

\[[1/(160\,\mu{\rm s})+1/(50\,\mu{\rm s})]^{-1}=38.1\,\mu\mathrm s.\]

The expected echo contrast is now \(e^{-10/38.1}=0.769\) and the ordinary Ramsey contrast \(0.349\). A single fitted \(T_2^*\) cannot display these three separate timescales. Measuring populations, Ramsey fringes and echoes on consistent ensembles gives three complementary observables with independently interpretable limits.

For a time-dependent classical detuning \(\delta(t)\), pulse sequences choose a modulation \(y(t)=\pm1\) multiplying the noise phase. The phase error is

\[\phi=\int y(t)\delta(t)dt.\]

For stationary zero-mean Gaussian noise, \(\langle e^{-i\phi}\rangle=e^{-\langle\phi^2\rangle/2}\) and the variance is a double integral of the noise correlation weighted by \(y(t)y(t')\). Equivalently it is the spectral integral of the power density weighted by \(|\int y(t)e^{i\omega t}dt|^2\), with the normalisation set by the chosen two-sided Fourier convention. A single echo has zero weight at \(\omega=0\) because its two half integrals cancel, while keeping finite-frequency sensitivity. Families of pulse spacings can therefore probe noise timescales rather than simply fitting larger and larger numbers to a nominal coherence time.

Find the damped saturation curve from optical Bloch equations

A continuously driven qubit has an additional route to separate population and coherence relaxation. In a rotating frame let \(H=\hbar(\Omega\sigma_x+\Delta\sigma_z)/2\), with \(z=p_e-p_g\), equilibrium \(z_{\rm eq}=-1\) at zero temperature, and transverse damping \(1/T_2\). Choose a consistent Bloch basis so the commutator gives the cross product of \((\Omega,0,\Delta)\) with \((x,y,z)\). The equations are

\[\dot x=-\Delta y-x/T_2,\]
\[\dot y=\Delta x-\Omega z-y/T_2,\]
\[\dot z=\Omega y-(z-z_{\rm eq})/T_1 .\]

At steady state the first equation gives

\[x=-\Delta T_2 y.\]

The second then gives

\[y=-\Omega T_2 z/(1+\Delta^2T_2^2).\]

Inserting into the third gives

\[z=z_{\rm eq}/[1+\Omega^2T_1T_2/(1+\Delta^2T_2^2)].\]

Thus the excited fraction for \(z_{\rm eq}=-1\) is

\[p_{e,\infty}=\frac{\Omega^2T_1T_2}{2[1+\Delta^2T_2^2+\Omega^2T_1T_2]}.\]

A weak resonant drive gives a population proportional to its power, and a strong continuous drive saturates toward one half rather than preparing a permanently inverted state. Coherent \(\pi\)-pulse inversion is a transient unitary process; continuous steady balance with relaxation gives a different preparation. The full angular-frequency width at half of the on-resonance excitation is \(2\sqrt{1+\Omega^2T_1T_2}/T_2\), so increasing power broadens the line even at unchanged intrinsic \(T_2\).

Choose \(T_1=20\) μs, \(T_2=16\) μs and resonant \(\Omega/2\pi=10\) kHz. The saturation parameter is

\[s=\Omega^2 T_1T_2=1.26.\]

The stationary excited fraction is \(s/[2(1+s)]=0.279\), and the ordinary-frequency full width is

\[\sqrt{1+s}/(\pi T_2)=29.9\,\mathrm{kHz}.\]

At very low drive it would be 19.9 kHz. A measured linewidth that increases approximately as the square root of power at high drive can have this coherent saturation origin without any increase in the environment's pure-dephasing rate. Comparing the zero-power linewidth extrapolation with an independent time-domain \(T_2\) is a stronger test than fitting a single apparent line broadening.

Connect an individual event stream to an ensemble lifetime

The lifetime \(T_1\) of one excited qubit is a distribution of observed emission times across many trials. With perfect detection, no drive and initially excited state, survival \(S(t)=e^{-\Gamma t}\) implies waiting-time density \(w(t)=-dS/dt=\Gamma e^{-\Gamma t}\). The mean waiting time and standard deviation are both \(1/\Gamma\). A no-click trajectory stays in the excited state conditionally until its first emission when the initial preparation is certainly excited. Its unnormalised weight decays exponentially, but assigning every no-click trajectory a continuously decreasing normalised excitation would confuse conditional and unconditioned statements. For an initial superposition the absence record instead changes the normalised state as derived earlier.

Real acquisitions may stop each trial at a fixed deadline \(T_{\rm stop}\). Treating all no-click trials as though they emitted exactly at the deadline biases lifetime inference. For \(n\) observed emissions at times \(t_j\) and \(m\) censored trials with no emission through \(T_{\rm stop}\), the likelihood is proportional to \(\prod_{j=1}^n\Gamma e^{-\Gamma t_j}\prod_{k=1}^m e^{-\Gamma T_{\rm stop}}\). Optimising gives

\[\hat\Gamma=n/(\sum_j t_j+mT_{\rm stop}).\]

The numerator is actual events and the denominator all time observed at risk. For 40 detections totalling 480 μs and 10 additional censored runs each observed 30 μs, the estimate is \(40/780=0.0513\,\mu\mathrm s^{-1}\), or \(T_1\simeq19.5\,\mu\mathrm s\). Discarding the censored runs instead would report 12 μs from the mean detected delay. This statistical correction follows directly from the same physical survival law used for the quantum channel.

Detection inefficiency can cause a different data problem. In a single-excitation undriven trial, a missed decay leaves a qubit in its ground state and unable to emit again. The probability of no registered event up to \(t\) is \(1-\eta[1-e^{-\Gamma t}]\) at constant efficiency \(\eta\), with a nonzero late plateau \(1-\eta\). It is not \(e^{-\eta\Gamma t}\), which would describe thinning a renewable stationary Poisson event stream. Long-time survival of the recorded count and independent final-state readout can distinguish a missed emission from a genuinely long-lived excitation. This example shows why the detector model must specify whether events can repeat after one has been missed.

Audit a reconstructed Pauli noise channel

Suppose three independently prepared Bloch components appear to shrink by factors \(\lambda_x,\lambda_y,\lambda_z\) after a calibrated waiting time, with no displacement of the centre. A diagonal unital qubit channel can be written \(\mathcal E(\rho)=\sum_{\nu=0,x,y,z}p_\nu\sigma_\nu\rho\sigma_\nu\) for a physical Pauli mixture. Expanding its action on the three axes and inverting gives

\[p_0=(1+\lambda_x+\lambda_y+\lambda_z)/4,\]
\[p_x=(1+\lambda_x-\lambda_y-\lambda_z)/4,\]

with cyclic partners for \(p_y,p_z\). Complete positivity demands all four numbers be nonnegative. Checking each \(|\lambda_i|\le1\) only checks lengths along single Bloch axes and, for this diagonal map, positivity on a single qubit. It does not guarantee that the reconstructed operation is consistent on half of an entangled system.

For \(\lambda=(0.9,0.9,0.5)\), every axis appears to shrink, yet \(p_z=(1-0.9-0.9+0.5)/4=-0.075\). Thus the exact diagonal unital channel inferred from those numbers is not completely positive. Finite measurement noise, neglected affine offsets or changing preparation could be responsible. Compare the fit with uncertainty regions and a constrained process model; simply forcing each measured contraction coefficient into \([-1,1]\) cannot repair this violation. As a constructive control, \(\lambda=(0.8,0.7,0.6)\) gives \(p_0=0.775,\ p_x=0.125,\ p_y=0.075,\ p_z=0.025\), all valid and summing to one. A simulator using these four random Pauli operations on entangled inputs then supplies a direct check of the estimated channel beyond single-qubit positivity.

Test how a coherence measurement depends on an ensemble

Compare quasistatic frequency spread with homogeneous decay in a Ramsey sequence. Identify which further measurement could distinguish the mechanisms.

Open this experiment online

Prove when two different event descriptions give the same channel

A given unconditioned quantum operation does not determine a unique set of environmental records. Suppose a set of Kraus operators \(K_j\) describes a channel. If an environment detector resolves an alternative orthonormal basis related by unitary \(U=(u_{\alpha j})\), its new system operators are \(\tilde K_\alpha=\sum_j u_{\alpha j}K_j\). Summing the unnormalised conditional outputs gives

\[\sum_{\alpha,j,k}u_{\alpha j}u_{\alpha k}^*K_j\rho K_k^\dagger=\sum_jK_j\rho K_j^\dagger.\]

This calculation explains why environment-dependent state trajectories can be quite different while every unconditioned prediction on the system agrees. A simulator's choice of individual jump records only describes a particular monitoring scheme when the environment observable actually corresponds to those records.

Consider complete dephasing in the \(z\) basis. One representation is \(K_0=|0\rangle\langle0|,\ K_1=|1\rangle\langle1|\), an ideal path measurement with its result forgotten. Rotate the two environment labels by the Hadamard matrix to obtain

\[\tilde K_+=(I/\sqrt2),\ \tilde K_-=(\sigma_z/\sqrt2).\]

These describe equally likely identity or phase-flip operations. For input \(|+x\rangle\), the first recorded instrument conditionally gives \(|0\rangle\) or \(|1\rangle\); the second gives \(|+x\rangle\) or \(|-x\rangle\). Both produce \(I/2\) if their outcome is ignored. For initial \(|0\rangle\) the first record is deterministic, while the second remains equally random and leaves the state unchanged up to a phase. Measuring only the system after discarding the labels cannot tell which set was used, but correlations with a retained environmental measurement can immediately tell them apart.

The same distinction is useful for a partially dephasing environment. If \(|0\rangle,|1\rangle\) become correlated with environment states of real overlap \(c\), the qubit's transverse Bloch vector contracts by \(c\). For \(-1\le c\le1\) a random phase-flip representation has probabilities \(p_I=(1+c)/2,\ p_Z=(1-c)/2\). For \(c<0\) a fringe phase reversal accompanies partial contraction; a reported positive “visibility” loses that sign. One can separate a deterministic phase rotation from the magnitude of random or entangling dephasing if the phase reference is maintained. Over independent identical intervals a real \(c\) multiplies repeatedly; the coherence after \(n\) steps is \(c^n\). If the same randomly chosen phase flip is instead held fixed throughout all intervals, successive errors are correlated and the even-step coherence can return to one. A single-step channel tomography cannot identify the environmental memory shared across steps.

Tell apart a memoryless phase-flip channel and a fixed hidden phase

Both devices individually implement \(p_Z=0.20\) over a calibrated interval for freshly prepared qubits. In device A the phase-flip draws at successive intervals are independent. In device B one draw is held fixed for both consecutive intervals. Predict \(+x\) readout after one and two intervals for an initial \(|+x\rangle\).

Derive and check the result

One interval leaves \(x=1-2p_Z=0.60\), so both devices give

\[P(+x)=0.80.\]

Independence in A gives \(x=0.60^2=0.36\) after two intervals, or \(P(+x)=0.68\). For B, two identical phase flips multiply to identity whether the hidden choice was identity or \(Z\), hence \(P(+x)=1\). In both devices the one-step process applied to an independently prepared ensemble is consistent with the measured completely positive map. Composing that map twice implicitly assumed fresh, independent environment conditions; a memory experiment tests exactly that assumption. Observing a revival thus need not imply an energy input, a negative probability or a nonunitary failure in the larger coupled system.

Distinguish dipole coherence from an excited-state population lifetime

Consider a single initially excited emitter in a memoryless zero-temperature environment with radiative population decay \(\Gamma\) and no additional dephasing. The probability density of emitting at time \(t\ge0\) is \(\Gamma e^{-\Gamma t}\). A normalised single-photon temporal mode consistent with this ideal undistinguished radiative channel is \(g(t)=\sqrt{\Gamma}\,e^{-\Gamma t/2}e^{-i\omega_0t}\) on \(t\ge0\). Its field amplitude decays twice as slowly as the population probability. Use the Fourier convention \(\tilde g(\omega)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\int g(t)e^{i\omega t}dt\). The half-line exponential integral gives

\[|\tilde g(\omega)|^2=\frac{\Gamma}{2\pi[(\Gamma/2)^2+(\omega-\omega_0)^2]} .\]

It integrates to one in angular frequency. At a detuning of \(\Gamma/2\) the intensity has half its peak, so the full angular-frequency linewidth is \(\Gamma\), or \(\Gamma/(2\pi)\) in hertz. Using the population decay directly as a field amplitude would predict a linewidth twice too large and the wrong wave-packet envelope. The transform-limited result requires a single radiative mode in the sense of a pure emitted temporal state and absence of additional phase randomisation. Extra pure dephasing broadens the spectrum while population decay may remain unchanged.

For \(\Gamma^{-1}=10.0\) ns the ideal linewidth is 15.9 MHz. Add independent homogeneous pure dephasing at \(\Gamma_\phi=2.0\times10^7\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}\). The first-order dipole correlation decays at \(\Gamma/2+\Gamma_\phi\), giving a full line width \((\Gamma/2+\Gamma_\phi)/\pi=22.3\) MHz. The photon detection time distribution can still have 10 ns decay. A measured line twice its nominal transform-limited width might indicate pure dephasing, but spatially varying frequencies, slow source drift or spectral resolution can also broaden the observed distribution. A lifetime measurement alone does not calibrate those effects.

The acquisition window has an independently calculable consequence. If only photons arriving before time \(T\) are retained, their normalised temporal mode is a truncated exponential divided by \(\sqrt{1-e^{-\Gamma T}}\) in this ideal pure-emission model. Its Fourier amplitude gains factor \(1-e^{-[\Gamma/2-i(\omega-\omega_0)]T}\) in the numerator. At \(T\ll1/\Gamma\) the shape approaches the squared sinc of a short rectangular temporal window. Selecting only early photons can thus broaden the spectrum even though the emitter and its environment are unchanged. A time gate, an energy filter and unconditioned counting describe different optical preparations and different uncertainty budgets.

Infer the transform-limited fraction from a linewidth and test the model

A homogeneous line has measured weak-drive full width 25.0 MHz and population lifetime 10.0 ns. Compute \(T_2\) and the implied pure dephasing rate assuming ideal Lorentzian broadening. Which second measurement would discriminate this inference from static sample inhomogeneity?

Derive and check the result

With angular half width \(1/T_2\), the frequency full width is \(1/(\pi T_2)\), so \(T_2=12.73\) ns. Subtracting \(1/(2T_1)=5.00\times10^7\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}\) from \(1/T_2=7.85\times10^7\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}\) gives

\[\Gamma_\phi=2.85\times10^7\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}.\]

The coherence relative to the radiative limit is

\[T_2/(2T_1)=0.637.\]

Compare a time-domain echo, a single-emitter linewidth or spectra over short acquisition segments with the ensemble line. A recoverable component of contrast or slowly wandering line centres would indicate a static or slow contribution instead of assigning every megahertz to homogeneous phase damping.

Tutorial · distinguish different sources of coherence loss

Recover a pure-dephasing rate and assess a conflicting fit

A population measurement gives

\[T_1=30.0\,\mu\mathrm s;\]

homogeneous Ramsey contrast gives

\[T_2=24.0\,\mu\mathrm s.\]

Find \(T_\phi\). What would a reported \(T_2=80\,\mu\mathrm s\) imply in the same model?

Work through the solution

Subtract \(1/(60\,\mu{\rm s})\) from \(1/(24\,\mu{\rm s})\) to get \(\Gamma_\phi=0.0250\,\mu{\rm s}^{-1}\), hence \(T_\phi=40.0\,\mu\mathrm s\). Replacing 24 by 80 yields a negative pure dephasing rate, incompatible with the specified independent time-homogeneous channels. Check the fitted envelope, uncertainties, thermally shifted equilibrium population and whether the two times were measured under comparable conditions. A negative fitted number is evidence about the model or data interpretation, not a physically negative random-event rate.

Calculate an inefficient no-click update

The initial excited probability is one half, the decay probability for an initially excited qubit during the window is 0.40, and a true emitted excitation is detected with efficiency 0.75. Find the probability of no click, its conditional excited fraction and the unconditioned population.

Work through the solution

A real emission occurs with probability \(0.5\times0.40=0.20\); a click is seen with probability 0.15, so no click has probability 0.85. Remaining excited and no click occurs with probability \(0.5(1-0.40)=0.30\). Conditional excitation is \(0.30/0.85=0.3529\); the unconditioned excitation is 0.30 since clicked branches are ground. For an initially coherent preparation the no-click off-diagonal term must also be updated by grouping the undetected-emission and true-no-emission Kraus outcomes. Simply replacing \(p\) by detector efficiency times \(p\) would predict the wrong physical excitation loss.

Diagnose a nonphysical time step and repair it

Apply one forward-Euler step to \(\dot p_1=-\Gamma p_1\) with initially excited state and \(\Gamma\Delta t=1.2\). Compute the prediction and an exact completely positive alternative. Would enforcing unit trace fix Euler's failure?

Work through the solution

Euler gives \(p_1=1-1.2=-0.2,\ p_0=1.2\), already with exact trace one. The finite-time amplitude-damping channel has \(p=1-e^{-1.2}=0.6988\), giving \(p_1=0.3012\). For this simple model the exact Kraus step can be applied repeatedly at arbitrary duration. In a driven model an operator-splitting scheme with unitary Hamiltonian substeps and completely positive damping substeps maintains positivity while introducing a controllable splitting error. Check convergence by halving the step and comparing with an independent observable. Renormalising trace or clipping a negative eigenvalue changes the numerical trajectory without diagnosing the original time-step error.

Recover a transition frequency from two separated pulses

Computational quantum experiments

A two-level system is driven by two short pulses with a free evolution interval between them. Determine why a population measurement carries phase information. Use a scan at several delays to separate a constant detuning, intrinsic coherence loss and trial-to-trial frequency variation.

Follow the amplitudes through both pulses

Use basis \(|g\rangle,|e\rangle\). Let the first pulse prepare \((|g\rangle+|e\rangle)/\sqrt2\) in the frame of the applied oscillator. A detuning \(\Delta\) in radians per second accumulates a relative phase \(\Delta\tau\) in free time \(\tau\). Immediately before the second pulse each state has population one half; measuring population here cannot reveal detuning. Choose a second pulse equivalent to projecting onto \((|g\rangle+e^{-i\phi}|e\rangle)/\sqrt2\). Then one output population is

\[P(\tau,\phi)=\tfrac12[1+\cos(\Delta\tau-\phi)].\]

The sign of the phase follows our definitions of detuning and the reference pulse. Shifting the reference phase makes it possible to check that sign. A measurement using only \(\phi=0\) cannot distinguish \(\Delta\) from \(-\Delta\). Coherence is the off-diagonal density-matrix element that the second pulse converts into an observable population difference.

Derive two envelopes with different origins

A homogeneous decay time \(T_2\) multiplies the off-diagonal element by \(e^{-\tau/T_2}\). If instead the detuning of each experimental shot is constant during that shot and drawn from a Gaussian distribution of mean \(\bar\Delta\) and standard deviation \(\sigma_\Delta\), average the complex phase, not its absolute value. Completing the square in the Gaussian integral gives

\[\langle e^{i\Delta\tau}\rangle=e^{i\bar\Delta\tau}e^{-\sigma_\Delta^2\tau^2/2}.\]

With both independent mechanisms the measured contrast is \(C(\tau)=\exp[-\tau/T_2-\sigma_\Delta^2\tau^2/2]\). One mechanism gives an initial logarithmic slope, the other adds curvature. A single low contrast measured at just one delay cannot establish which one caused it.

Infer parameters from quadratures and a frequency scan

Record the \(\phi=0,\pi/2\) outputs, then form \(X=2P_0-1=C\cos(\bar\Delta\tau)\) and \(Y=2P_{\pi/2}-1=C\sin(\bar\Delta\tau)\). Their length estimates contrast and their angle estimates accumulated phase modulo \(2\pi\). Suppose at \(0.50\,\mathrm{ms}\) the readings are 0.500 and 0.850. Then \(X=0,\ Y=0.700\), and the phase is \(\pi/2\) modulo \(2\pi\). With an independent bound \(|\bar\Delta/2\pi|<1\,\mathrm{kHz}\), the implied cyclic detuning is \(+500\,\mathrm{Hz}\). Longer delays resolve fine frequency differences at the cost of phase ambiguity and declining contrast. Use a shorter-delay record to unwrap a longer one.

At a contrast of 0.700 the relation \(-\ln(0.700)=\tau/T_2+\sigma_\Delta^2\tau^2/2\) supplies one equation for two unknowns. Several delays, with independent error bars, are needed to identify the linear and quadratic terms. At very low signal the length \(\sqrt{X^2+Y^2}\) has a positive noise bias. Fitting the two directly measured quadratures can be preferable to fitting the length.

Explore two mechanisms

The graph holds the homogeneous \(T_2\) at 2 ms. It lets you set signed cyclic detuning and shot-to-shot cyclic frequency spread. Compare both quadrature populations with the upper contrast envelope. First reverse the sign of the detuning, then increase the spread without changing the mean. Which effect interchanges peaks of one quadrature and which damps both?

Tutorial

What is the fringe spacing in a scan of cyclic detuning at fixed delay of 0.80 ms?

Work through the solution

One full phase cycle needs \(\delta f\,\tau=1\). The spacing is

\[1/0.00080=1250\,\mathrm{Hz}.\]

The angular-frequency spacing is \(2\pi\) times larger.

A fixed delay gives

\[P_0=P_{\pi/2}=0.5.\]

Does that show a phase of 45 degrees?

Work through the solution

Both centred quadratures are zero, so the contrast is unresolved and the angle is undefined. A phase of 45 degrees with finite contrast gives equal, nonzero centred quadratures.

Explain how a refocusing pulse halfway through the evolution helps identify quasistatic detuning noise.

Work through the solution

The pulse exchanges the phase-accumulating states, reversing phase winding for a detuning that stays constant through both halves. Random variations during the interval need not cancel. Recovering contrast in the refocused sequence supports a slowly varying contribution to the original envelope.

Compare a continuous drive in the transition chapter with the role of the second pulse here.

Liouville flow and what an ensemble can remember

Classical mechanics

A cloud of identical noninteracting beads is released with slightly different initial positions and momenta. Its position distribution widens. Does the system occupy more phase space, or has its uncertainty merely changed shape?

Follow a small phase-space element

For one canonical degree of freedom the velocity of a point \((q,p)\) is \((\partial_pH,-\partial_qH)\). A short-time map takes \(\boldsymbol z\) to \(\boldsymbol z+\boldsymbol v(\boldsymbol z,t)\,dt\). Its Jacobian matrix is \(I+(\partial\boldsymbol v/\partial\boldsymbol z)dt\); expanding the determinant gives \(1+(\nabla_z\cdot\boldsymbol v)dt+O(dt^2)\). Hamilton's equations make the divergence zero because the two mixed second derivatives of a smooth Hamiltonian cancel. Each additional canonical pair contributes a similar cancellation. A time-dependent Hamiltonian may change energy but still preserve the instantaneous phase-space volume along the flow.

Conservation of the number of ensemble members in a moving phase-space box also gives the continuity equation \(\partial_t f+\nabla_z\cdot(f\boldsymbol v)=0\). The vanishing divergence leaves \(df/dt=0\) on each trajectory. A stationary mechanical system can admit \(f=F(H)\) as a stationary distribution because trajectories stay on energy surfaces. Such stationarity alone does not imply that one trajectory explores an entire energy surface. For example, further integrals of motion restrict regular multidimensional trajectories.

Propagate a cloud released into free flight

For mass \(m\), the exact map is \(p=p_0,\ q=q_0+tp_0/m\). Its determinant is one regardless of time. An initially uncorrelated cloud with variances \(s_q^2,s_p^2\) has evolved covariance \(C_{qp}=ts_p^2/m\). The new position variance is \(s_q^2+(ts_p/m)^2\), while the momentum variance stays fixed. Compute the determinant of this covariance matrix:

\[\sigma_q^2\sigma_p^2-C_{qp}^2=s_q^2s_p^2 .\]

For a Gaussian the ellipse at a fixed multiple of the covariance radius thus retains its area while it shears. With \(m=1\,\mathrm{kg}\), \(s_q=1\,\mathrm m\), \(s_p=0.5\,\mathrm{kg\,m/s}\), and \(t=4\,\mathrm s\), the observed position standard deviation is \(\sqrt5=2.236\,\mathrm m\). The momentum standard deviation remains 0.5 and the covariance becomes \(1.0\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2/s}\). Their uncorrected width product is 1.118 in action units. The square root of the covariance determinant is still 0.5. Multiplication of measured marginal widths alone falsely suggests an area increase.

Compare reversible shear and real drag

Now apply a linear drag \(\dot p=-\gamma p\) to each bead with constant \(\gamma>0\). Its phase-space divergence is \(-\gamma\), hence the map determinant after time \(t\) is \(e^{-\gamma t}\). Direct integration gives

\[p=e^{-\gamma t}p_0,\ q=q_0+(1-e^{-\gamma t})p_0/(m\gamma).\]

The cloud now genuinely loses area in the reduced bead description. A larger Hamiltonian model of bead plus environment can remain volume preserving. Choose a drag rate in the graph and compare the original, freely sheared and damped ellipses for the same observation time. Check there is a smooth zero-drag limit rather than substituting zero into a quotient.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on distinguishing a shear from dissipation

A harmonic oscillator maps its scaled quadratures by a rotation. Show why an initially anisotropic cloud can periodically sharpen in position without phase-space cooling.

Work through the solution

Write its covariance as \(R\Sigma_0R^T\). Since \(\det R=1\), its determinant is unchanged. A rotation of one quarter turn exchanges the original widths, so position can sharpen when the original momentum width in scaled units was smaller. Energy extraction is unnecessary.

At \(t=3\,\mathrm s\) the measured ellipse area is 55 percent of the initial area in the linear-drag model. Infer \(\gamma\). Which independent observable checks the inference?

Work through the solution
\[\gamma=-\ln(0.55)/3=0.199\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}.\]

The ratio of final to initial momentum standard deviations should also be 0.55 for drag without random forcing. Added environmental fluctuations would modify that prediction.

Further reading · Cambridge classical dynamics, Hamiltonian flow and Liouville's theorem.

Time-dependent diffusion

Numerical partial differential equations

Derive the diffusion time-step restriction from two independent arguments and compare explicit and implicit decay of spatial modes.

From first principles

Derive the explicit heat step and its grid-dependent time scale

Take the heat equation \(T_t=\alpha T_{xx}\) with constant positive diffusivity on a uniform one-dimensional grid. Fourier conduction through each side of a cell produces a temperature update proportional to the difference of the two neighbour gradients. With forward Euler timing, collect those fluxes to obtain

\[T_i^{n+1}=T_i^n+r(T_{i-1}^n-2T_i^n+T_{i+1}^n),\]
\[r=\alpha\Delta t/h^2 .\]

When \(0\le r\le1/2\) the new temperature is a convex average of three old ones, preserving bounds on a source-free grid. To check whether a higher step could still be stable for all periodic grid disturbances, substitute \(T_j^n=g^n e^{ij\theta}\), where \(j\) is the node index. The amplification simplifies to

\[g=1+r(e^{-i\theta}-2+e^{i\theta})=1-4r\sin^2(\theta/2).\]

The alternating mode at \(\theta=\pi\) gives \(g=1-4r\), so \(|g|\le1\) again requires \(r\le1/2\). Halving spatial spacing therefore quarters the largest explicit time step for this discretization. Implicit stepping changes this restriction but still has to resolve any transient relevant to the measurement.

Derivation and applications

Can an energy-stable heat step create an alternating transient?

A diffusion mode should decay rather than oscillate. To distinguish decay of a norm from preservation of sign, examine the finite-element or finite-volume semidiscrete system \(M\dot u+Ku=0\), with symmetric positive definite mass matrix \(M\) and thermal stiffness \(K\) positive semidefinite. An eigenvector satisfying \(Kv=\lambda Mv\) has scalar amplitude \(y(t)\) obeying \(y'=-\lambda y\). A generalized \(\theta\) step applied to this mode gives

\[[1+\theta z]y^{n+1}=[1-(1-\theta)z]y^n,\]
\[z=\Delta t\,\lambda .\]

For Crank–Nicolson take \(\theta=1/2\). The factor \(G=(1-z/2)/(1+z/2)\) has \(|G|\le1\) for all \(z\ge0\) but changes sign when \(z>2\). An initial grid-scale component can therefore alternate in time. To test a remedy, replace the first full step by two half backward-Euler steps. Their combined factor is

\[G_{\rm start}=(1+z/2)^{-2}.\]

Thereafter return to the second-order Crank–Nicolson update. After \(n\) further full steps the mode amplitudes, with and without the initial damping, are

\[y_{\rm damped}=G_{\rm start}G^ny^0,\]
\[y_{\rm plain}=G^{n+1}y^0 .\]

For small \(z\), both startup strategies agree through first order, while for large \(z\) the two half steps strongly suppress the unresolved mode. This argument explains a startup treatment for nonsmooth initial data; sign preservation of every physical grid value is a stronger matrix property than sign preservation of one mode.

Worked example

Check damping of a near-grid-scale eigenmode

Take \(\lambda=2000\) s⁻¹ and \(\Delta t=0.0100\) s. Then \(z=20\). A single Crank–Nicolson step leaves \(-9/11=-0.818\) of the amplitude; two backward-Euler half steps leave \(1/121=0.00826\). The exact amplitude factor is

\[e^{-20}=2.06\times10^{-9}.\]

Both discrete results are bounded, but the startup treatment removes almost all of a mode that the time grid cannot resolve.

Second application

Check the cost for a physically slow component

For the same mesh and step, a slow mode with \(\lambda=2.00\) s⁻¹ has \(z=0.0200\). Crank–Nicolson gives \(0.99/1.01=0.980198\), the startup gives \(1/1.01^2=0.980296\) and the exact factor is \(0.980199\). The slow component changes by less than \(10^{-4}\) in this comparison. A timestep must still resolve a transient that is itself part of the desired output.

Discretise space and time separately

A heat pulse in a thin insulated rod smooths over time according to \(u_t=D u_{xx}\) for constant positive diffusivity \(D\). Use a uniform grid of spacing \(h\), and initially examine interior points or periodic boundaries so the stencil is unchanged everywhere. Replacing the second derivative by a centred difference gives a system of ordinary differential equations for the nodal temperatures. The time method still has to resolve the fastest rate that this spatial operator can generate.

An explicit Euler step yields \(u_i^{n+1}=r u_{i-1}^n+(1-2r)u_i^n+r u_{i+1}^n\) with \(r=D\Delta t/h^2\). If \(0\leq r\leq1/2\), the update is a convex combination of three temperatures. The maximum cannot rise and the minimum cannot fall in a source-free interior step. Starting with one hot node at 1 and neighbours at zero, \(r=0.4\) gives a centre value 0.2 and neighbours 0.4 after a single step. At \(r=0.6\) the centre would become negative although the continuum heat equation preserves nonnegativity.

Identify the spatial modes that set the limit

Insert \(u_j^n=g^n e^{\mathrm{i}j\theta}\), where \(\mathrm{i}\) is the imaginary unit and \(j\) indexes the grid. The two neighbouring values contribute factors \(e^{\pm\mathrm{i}\theta}\), yielding \(g=1-4r\sin^2(\theta/2)\). Long waves have \(\sin^2(\theta/2)\simeq\theta^2/4\), so their decay approaches the continuum rate as the mesh is refined. The alternating grid mode \(\theta=\pi\) has the most negative amplification and enforces \(r\leq1/2\) for bounded magnitude.

At the limiting value \(r=1/2\) the alternating mode flips sign without losing amplitude. A strict margin damps it. For \(D=10^{-4}\) m²/s and \(h=2\) mm, the maximum explicit step is

\[h^2/(2D)=0.020\,\mathrm{s}.\]

Refining to 1 mm reduces that limit to 0.005 s. The mesh gives a sharper temperature profile yet can cost four times more steps over the same physical interval, besides the increased number of spatial degrees of freedom.

Compare backward Euler and Crank–Nicolson

Writing the semidiscrete system as \(u'=-A_hu\), backward Euler solves \((I+\Delta t A_h)u^{n+1}=u^n\). Crank–Nicolson averages the old and new diffusive fluxes, giving \((I+\Delta t A_h/2)u^{n+1}=(I-\Delta t A_h/2)u^n\). For a Fourier mode put \(s=4r\sin^2(\theta/2)\). Their factors are \(1/(1+s)\) and \((1-s/2)/(1+s/2)\), respectively. Both remain bounded at arbitrarily large \(s\), but Crank–Nicolson may alternate for under-resolved sharp features.

With \(s=20\), backward Euler retains only \(1/21\) per step. Crank–Nicolson yields \(-9/11\), retaining much more of the fast component and reversing its sign. For a smooth low-frequency mode Crank–Nicolson generally gives more accurate temporal decay because it is second order. Decide whether early sharp transients or later smooth behaviour is the relevant physical quantity. To identify spatial and temporal error separately, refine \(\Delta t\) first on a fixed mesh, then refine space with time error kept well below spatial error. Merely halving both at once cannot reveal which term dominated the original discrepancy.

Apply a prescribed heat input without confusing it with temperature

An insulated periodic rod with uniform heating rate \(s\) measured in K/s has an average temperature that must rise by \(s\Delta t\) each step. Sum the centred second differences over the periodic indices: each nodal value appears with coefficients \(1,-2,1\), which cancel. The source alone changes the mean. For \(s=3\) K/s and a step of 0.02 s the numerical average must increase by 0.06 K, independently of the diffusivity and initial profile.

A code can pass this global test while still mishandling short-wave damping, since an alternating error has zero spatial mean. Pair a mean-temperature test with a nonconstant mode-decay test. Use a homogeneous periodic run for isolates of diffusion, and a uniform temperature with prescribed source for the storage term. A failure in one test then identifies a different part of the mathematical balance than a failure in the other.

Prove decay of a discrete thermal energy

Let a closed semidiscrete diffusion model be \(M\dot u+Ku=0\), with \(M\) symmetric positive definite and \(K\) symmetric positive semidefinite. Assume \(K{\bf1}=0\), expressing zero flux for a uniform temperature. Backward Euler satisfies \(M(u^{n+1}-u^n)+\Delta t Ku^{n+1}=0\). Multiplying by the constant vector and using symmetry gives

\[{\bf1}^TMu^{n+1}={\bf1}^TMu^n.\]

Thus the capacity-weighted sum, proportional to stored sensible heat for constant capacities and a fixed reference, is conserved for any step size.

Now multiply by \(u^{n+1}\). The identity \(a^TM(a-b)=[\|a\|_M^2-\|b\|_M^2+\|a-b\|_M^2]/2\) yields the exact balance

\[\tfrac12\|u^n\|_M^2-\tfrac12\|u^{n+1}\|_M^2=\Delta t\,(u^{n+1})^TKu^{n+1}+\tfrac12\|u^{n+1}-u^n\|_M^2 .\]

Both terms are nonnegative. The quadratic quantity therefore decays although total sensible heat is conserved. Subtracting the conserved mean first yields the same decay statement for temperature variation around equilibrium. The first term represents spatial smoothing measured at the new time, and the second reflects the numerical time update. This derivation gives a stability certificate on nonuniform grids without assuming Fourier modes.

Audit two cells with different heat capacities

Choose capacities 1 and 3 J/K, conductance 2 W/K, and initial temperature deviations \((4,0)^T\) K. The conserved sensible heat is 4 J, so the final equilibrium in a closed system must be one kelvin above reference. At \(\Delta t=0.5\) s the matrix \(M+\Delta tK\) has rows \((2,-1)\) and \((-1,4)\), and the right-hand side is \((4,0)^T\). The new temperatures are \((16/7,4/7)^T\). Checking their weighted sum gives \(16/7+3(4/7)=4\), as required. The unweighted average changes, which is physically appropriate for unequal capacities.

The quadratic quantity at the start is \(4^2/2=8\). After the step it is

\[[(16/7)^2+3(4/7)^2]/2=152/49.\]

Its drop is \(240/49\). The conductive contribution is \((0.5)(2)(12/7)^2=144/49\); the time-update contribution is

\[[(-12/7)^2+3(4/7)^2]/2=96/49.\]

Their sum recovers the measured drop exactly. Neither contribution equals a loss of the 4 J of sensible heat. The quadratic measure depends on squared temperatures and has different physical units.

A positive heat supply or a prescribed exterior temperature introduces a boundary or source work term into these identities. Applying the closed-system decay test to an externally heated experiment would therefore diagnose the wrong physical model. Verify which conserved or dissipated quantity is actually implied by the capacity matrix, boundary conditions and source terms before comparing numerical integrators.

The conserved mean suggests another check after many large steps. The steady state must have zero conductance dissipation, which for two connected cells requires equal temperatures. Together with the weighted heat total this fixes both temperatures at one kelvin above reference. A scheme that instead approaches the unweighted initial average of two would be converging to the wrong conserved quantity despite producing a visually smooth, uniform endpoint. The measured quadratic variation about the correct mean tends to zero; the uncentred squared quantity tends to a positive constant because the remaining uniform state still has temperature above the chosen reference.

What happens to a grid-scale temperature ripple?

The alternating spatial mode on a uniform heat grid has discrete Laplacian eigenvalue \(-4/\Delta x^2\). Substitute this mode into each method and compare the amplitude after one step. The plot isolates the shortest resolvable pattern.

What a periodic grid does to a decaying Fourier mode

Numerical partial differential equations

A periodic diffusion solver predicts the shape of a sampled wave while its amplitude decays too slowly. Fourier analysis separates spatial differentiation error, an unresolved physical mode and errors caused by the time integrator.

Diagonalise the differential operator on the periodic interval

Use a dimensionless coordinate \(0\le x<2\pi\) and solve \(u_t=\kappa u_{xx}\) with periodic values and periodic derivatives. The complex exponentials \(e^{imx}\), with integer m, satisfy both conditions and are eigenfunctions of the second derivative. Put \(u(x,t)=A_m(t)e^{imx}\) and cancel the common exponential. The scalar amplitude equation is

\[A'_m=-\kappa m^2 A_m.\]

Thus every cosine or sine mode decays by \(\exp(-\kappa m^2t)\), with no phase shift, while the m=0 mean is conserved.

On a uniform grid \(x_j=2\pi j/N\), the samples of \(m\) and \(m+\ell N\) agree for every integer \(\ell\). A discrete Fourier transform returns coefficients in one representative band. For the real cosine test with even N we can fold a positive physical mode into \(k=\min(r,N-r)\), \(r=m\bmod N\). A Fourier spectral Laplacian multiplies that coefficient by \(-k^2\). For a resolved mode it differentiates the trigonometric interpolant exactly, up to arithmetic. For unresolved data it differentiates the aliased representative; no operation using only those samples can identify the original m.

Apply the centred difference stencil to a single wave

The standard second difference is \((u_{j+1}-2u_j+u_{j-1})/h^2\), \(h=2\pi/N\). Put the grid wave \(u_j=e^{ikjh}\) into the stencil. Factor out the common phase and simplify with \(e^{i\xi}+e^{-i\xi}=2\cos\xi\). This gives the symbol and its small-h expansion:

\[{e^{ikh}-2+e^{-ikh}\over h^2}=-{4\over h^2}\sin^2(kh/2)=-k^2+{k^4h^2\over12}+O(k^6h^4).\]

The effective squared wavenumber is smaller than \(k^2\) for every nonconstant mode in the resolved band, so this spatial approximation decays too slowly if the time equation is integrated exactly. The error is modest when \(kh\ll1\), but at the Nyquist mode \(kh=\pi\) the ratio is

\[4/\pi^2=0.4053.\]

A stable time integrator cannot repair this spatial attenuation error without additional modeling. The experiment exponentiates both discretizations' symbols exactly, isolating space and sampling here.

Measure separate errors with the same sampled initial data

Choose \(\kappa=0.5,\ m=3,\ N=16\). The true squared wavenumber is 9 and the difference stencil gives 8.006. Starting at unit amplitude, at \(t=0.20\) exact and spectral amplitudes are 0.40657; finite differences give 0.44906. Doubling N to 32 changes the difference result to 0.41716. The absolute amplitude error falls from 0.04249 to 0.01059, a ratio of 4.01 consistent with the second-order expansion. This is a spatial-convergence study because time evolution introduces no truncation error.

Now take \(m=12,\ N=16\). Every sampled cosine equals the low mode \(k=4\) at \(t=0\). At \(t=0.02\), the continuum amplitude for the true physical mode is \(e^{-1.44}=0.237\), whereas the spectral grid predicts

\[e^{-0.16}=0.852.\]

A spectral computation can therefore agree exactly with its trigonometric interpolant and disagree strongly with the evolution of the intended signal. Sampling on a finer grid or obtaining prior bandwidth information resolves that ambiguity. The mean mode is particularly revealing when a nonconstant physical cosine aliases to a constant.

Start at mode 3 and successively double N from 8 to 32. Compute two amplitude-error ratios at the same time. Then use mode 12 on 16 points to observe an alias, and increase N until the Fourier grid represents the intended mode.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · resolve a damping rate to a target tolerance

For a mode well inside the grid bandwidth, estimate the number of samples per wavelength needed for a relative error below 1% in the decay rate with centred differences. Test the estimate for \(m=2\). How could the time integrator contaminate a measurement of this spatial error?

Show the resolution estimate

Writing p for samples per wavelength gives

\[kh=2\pi/p.\]

The relative rate error is approximately \((kh)^2/12\). Requiring at most 0.01 gives \(p\gtrsim18.14\); try 19 samples per wavelength, or \(N=38\) for \(m=2\). The exact ratio \([\sin(\pi/19)/(\pi/19)]^2\) is about 0.991. A forward-Euler step would replace exponential propagation by \(1-\kappa k_{\rm eff}^2\Delta t\). Its repeated-step error could partly mask the underdamping of the spatial stencil. Compare with the exponential of the discrete symbol before attributing a measured rate to space alone.

Further reading · Lloyd N. Trefethen, Spectral Methods in MATLAB · periodic grids, Fourier differentiation and downloadable programs

Check the discrete eigenvalue with OneSimulation

A spatial sine also diagonalises the five-point Dirichlet Laplacian on a square. Use the same modified wave-number argument in both coordinate directions. Run python poisson-mode.py --mode 2 --cells 8 16 32 and compare the observed nodal error with the discrete eigenvalue prediction in the CSV. This is a steady forced problem whose spatial operator can be checked independently of any time integrator. Download the OneSimulation verification model.

Splitting a stiff relaxation from a slowly varying drive

Numerical ordinary differential equations

A sensor tries to follow a changing reference while a fast relaxation damps departures. An explicit method may oscillate wildly even though the underlying transient decays monotonically. Treating the restoring term implicitly can resolve the slow motion using steps longer than the transient time. Whether those steps measure the transient accurately is a different question.

Construct an exact benchmark with two time scales

Let \(s(t)=\sin t\) and choose a relaxation time \(\varepsilon>0\). Consider \(y'=s'(t)+(s(t)-y)/\varepsilon\), with \(y(0)=1\). Set \(e=y-s\). Subtraction of \(s'\) gives \(e'=-e/\varepsilon\), hence the reference solution \(y(t)=\sin t+e^{-t/\varepsilon}\). The slow reference changes over times of order one, while the initial mismatch decays significantly over \(\varepsilon\). We know both pieces exactly and can diagnose errors even when a stable result looks visually smooth.

Explicit Euler applied to a perturbation of the state multiplies that perturbation by \(1-r\), with \(r=h/\varepsilon\). It damps monotonically only for \(0<r\le1\), changes sign while damping for \(1<r<2\), and grows for \(r>2\). These statements compare nearby solutions at equal drive. The full signal also contains the time variation of \(s\). At the boundary \(r=2\) a state perturbation alternates without decaying. Refining the step to follow a slow input alone can therefore give the wrong choice of \(h\).

Solve only the fast part implicitly

Split \(f_{\rm slow}(t)=\cos t\) from \(f_{\rm fast}(t,y)=(\sin t-y)/\varepsilon\). Evaluate the slow part at \(t_n\) and the fast part at \(t_{n+1},y_{n+1}\). This first-order additive implicit-explicit Euler step needs one scalar linear solve:

\[(1+r)y_{n+1}=y_n+h\cos t_n+r\sin t_{n+1}.\]

The state perturbation now multiplies by \(1/(1+r)\). It is damped at every positive step and tends to zero as \(r\) grows. Subtract \(\sin t_{n+1}\) from both sides after solving. The mismatch obeys \(e_{n+1}=[e_n+h\cos t_n-(\sin t_{n+1}-\sin t_n)]/(1+r)\). The bracketed forcing defect is \(O(h^2)\) for smooth input. In the stiff limit at fixed \(h\), the endpoint approaches the current reference. Moving the reference in the fast term to the old time would instead drag it one sample behind in that limit. The choice of what to treat implicitly includes the time at which a fast equilibrium is imposed.

Test early-time accuracy and late-time stability

For \(\varepsilon=0.02\), \(h=0.05\), \(r=2.5\). At the first step the exact value is

\[\sin(0.05)+e^{-2.5}=0.1321.\]

Explicit Euler gives

\[1+0.05(1-50)=-1.45.\]

The implicit-explicit value is

\[[1+0.05+2.5\sin(0.05)]/3.5=0.3357.\]

The latter is stable but visibly overestimates the surviving transient. Its damping factor \(1/3.5=0.286\) differs substantially from \(e^{-2.5}=0.0821\). A question about peak rate or initial settling requires shorter steps even if the late-time reference is recovered.

The experiment holds the final time at one while the number of steps changes. The vertical coordinate is the base-ten logarithm of absolute error, with values below \(10^{-15}\) drawn at that floor. Negative heights therefore correspond to errors less than one. Track the maximum error separately from the final error as the transient dies out. Try an initially unstable explicit run, then double the number of steps. The stiffness criterion depends on the ratio \(h/\varepsilon\), while accuracy on the reference also depends on \(h\) itself.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on identifying which component is stiff

Keep \(h=0.04\) and reduce \(\varepsilon\) from 0.04 to 0.004. For each case compare exact, explicit and implicit amplification of a state perturbation. What quantity would force a reduction of \(h\) even with the implicit-explicit method?

Work through the solution

At \(r=1\) the three factors are \(e^{-1}=0.368\), zero and \(1/2\). At \(r=10\) they are \(4.54\times10^{-5}\), \(-9\) and \(1/11=0.0909\). For a measurement of the initial decay rate the implicit discretisation substantially misrepresents both exact factors. Bounding error during that transient requires smaller steps relative to \(\varepsilon\), even though its numerical amplification is stable.

Further reading · Ascher, Ruuth and Wetton, implicit-explicit time discretisations.

Learning a drifting temperature from a sequence of readings

Inverse problems and least squares

Derive the scalar Kalman update by completing the square and see how process uncertainty controls memory of the past.

Propagate a distribution through time

A drifting offset follows \(x_k=x_{k-1}+w_k\). A thermometer reports \(z_k=x_k+v_k\). Assume independent, zero-mean Gaussian process increments of variance \(Q\) and sensor noise of variance \(R\), independent at each time and of the initial state. Before the next reading, the mean stays at \(m^-=m_{k-1}\) and the variance adds, \(P^-=P_{k-1}+Q\).

The negative log posterior, up to constants, is \((x-m^-)^2/(2P^-)+(z_k-x)^2/(2R)\). Collect the coefficient of \(x^2\) and its linear term. The result can be written in two equivalent forms:

\[P_k=(1/P^-+1/R)^{-1},\]
\[m_k=m^-+K(z_k-m^-),\]
\[K=P^-/(P^-+R).\]

Thus the fraction of the innovation used is a model prediction. When sensors are noisy the gain falls. When the model permits rapid drift, uncertainty grows between readings, giving the new measurement a larger role. These are variances, so a stated standard deviation must be squared first.

A reading with units and a repeated reading

A prior says \(20.0\pm2.0\) °C (one standard deviation), with negligible intervening drift. A sensor of standard deviation 1 °C reads 23 °C. The gain is \(4/(4+1)=0.8\), giving \(22.4\) °C and variance \(0.8\) °C². A second independent reading of 23 °C gives gain \(0.8/1.8\) and mean \(22.6667\) °C. Treating a duplicate record as independent would falsely claim the same information gain.

In a long random walk with \(Q=0.25\) and \(R=1\) in squared units, a steady posterior variance satisfies

\[P=(P+Q)R/(P+Q+R).\]

Rearrangement gives \(P^2+QP-QR=0\), so the physically positive root is about 0.3904. New uncertainty replenishes the information squeezed out by measurement.

Follow a change in the underlying temperature

For a reproducible illustration, a fixed synthetic thermometer trace contains a smooth periodic disturbance and a step in the hidden temperature. It is deliberately held fixed while the filter's assumed \(Q\) and \(R\) change. Compare the lag just after the step with the wiggle once the temperature settles.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on noisy data over time

If \(Q=0\), show how \(n\) independent readings with the same variance affect precision.

Worked solution

Each update adds \(1/R\) to precision \(1/P\). Induction gives

\[1/P_n=1/P_0+n/R.\]

This is precisely the precision of a batch Gaussian update.

What pattern in the innovations would call the random-walk assumptions into question?

Worked solution

A long run of positive innovations after a systematic ramp suggests bias in the prediction. Serial correlation suggests missing dynamics or correlated observation errors. Check against predicted innovation variance \(P^-+R\) and examine signs and lags as well as their sizes.

Read the university treatment

Extend the scalar precision update to several state variables and identify the matrix made invertible by a positive observation variance. Stephen Boyd · Stanford EE363, estimation and the Kalman filter

Predicting a new measurement when the fitted parameters are correlated

Inverse problems and least squares

Propagate the full posterior covariance to a quantity of interest, distinguish latent and measured uncertainty and select the observation that reduces the uncertainty of the actual prediction.

One model supplies many possible targets

For parameters \(\theta\) with posterior mean \(m\) and covariance \(C\), a linear target \(s=h^T\theta\) has mean \(h^Tm\). Subtract the mean and expand the quadratic product to obtain

\[\operatorname{Var}(s)=h^TCh.\]

An independent sensor with variance \(R\) changes the variance of a future measured value to \(h^TCh+R\). Repeated fits of noisy datasets and repeated physical measurements are distinct sources of variation.

A candidate new observation \(z=a^T\theta+\epsilon\) with variance \(R_a\) has covariance \(h^TCa\) with the target. For a Gaussian posterior the conditional covariance update is \(C_+=C-Caa^TC/(a^TCa+R_a)\), found either by completing the square or projecting out the optimal multiple of the innovation. The target variance decreases by

\[\Delta\operatorname{Var}(s)=\frac{(h^TCa)^2}{a^TCa+R_a}.\]
Background · Posterior predictive
\[p(y_*|y)=\int p(y_*|m)p(m|y)\,dm\]

A distribution over parameters induces uncertainty in a new observation. Measurement noise and uncertainty of the prediction mean are distinct contributions.

A precise sum and an uncertain difference

Let \(C\) have diagonal entries 1 and correlation \(-0.9\). A target \(h=(1,1)\) has variance \(2-1.8=0.2\). The difference \(h=(1,-1)\) has variance 3.8. Ignoring correlations would predict 2 for both. With sensor variance 0.25, a newly measured sum has variance 0.45 although the latent sum variance remains 0.2.

For the target sum, measuring the first coordinate with variance 0.25 reduces variance by \((1-0.9)^2/(1+0.25)=0.008\). Measuring the sum itself at the same variance reduces it by \(0.2^2/(0.2+0.25)=0.0889\). These two sensors have differently normalised sensitivity vectors. Their stated noise levels refer to their own output units and must be calibrated before a cost comparison.

Choose the measurement direction

For a fairer geometric comparison, restrict both measurement and prediction directions to unit length. Fix the target along the normalised sum. Sweep candidate observation angles and read the remaining prediction variance. Change the parameter correlation: when does a coordinate sensor improve the target appreciably?

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on prediction and parameter recovery

If \(h^TCa=0\), can a noise-free measurement of \(a^T\theta\) improve the linear prediction under these Gaussian assumptions?

Worked solution

The covariance reduction is exactly zero. The measurement may determine another direction precisely while carrying no linear information about the target.

The posterior on \(\theta\) is strongly non-Gaussian. Which conclusions still hold exactly?

Worked solution

Mean and variance of a fixed linear prediction still follow from its first two moments, and independent sensor variance still adds. The Gaussian conditional update and an interval based only on two standard deviations need additional assumptions.

Read the university treatment

Compare the projection of a vector measurement with the covariance of a scalar target. Check the direction of each matrix product. Stephen Boyd · Stanford EE363, estimation

Symplectic transformations, Poisson brackets and the geometry of measurements

Analytical mechanics from constraints to invariants

Two phase-space plots can enclose the same volume while predicting different responses to an impulse. Derive the stronger condition that makes a change of variables canonical, test it on measured linear maps and use Poisson brackets to propagate observables without solving separately for every coordinate.

Derive the matrix condition from Hamilton's equations

Collect \(n\) canonical positions and momenta into \(z=(q_1,\ldots,q_n,p_1,\ldots,p_n)^T\). Write Hamilton's equations as \(\dot z=J\nabla_z H\), where \(J\) has zero diagonal blocks, identity upper right and minus identity lower left. For a time-independent change \(Z=f(z)\) with Jacobian \(S=\partial Z/\partial z\), the chain rule gives

\[\dot Z=S J S^T\nabla_Z H.\]

To preserve the canonical form for every differentiable Hamiltonian require

\[SJS^T=J.\]

For an invertible square matrix this is equivalent to \(S^TJS=J\). In geometric terms the bilinear antisymmetric form \(\omega(u,v)=u^TJv\) has the same value on any transported pair of tangent vectors.

Taking determinants gives \((\det S)^2=1\), and symplectic maps have positive determinant equal to one. One way to see the sign is to take the top exterior power of the preserved two-form: \(\omega^n\) fixes an oriented volume. In one canonical pair the identity \(S^TJS=(\det S)J\) makes determinant one sufficient. In two or more pairs a single volume condition cannot enforce every entry of the matrix condition. For example in ordering \((q_1,q_2,p_1,p_2)\), scaling by diagonal entries \((2,1,1,1/2)\) preserves four-dimensional volume. But \(\{Q_1,P_1\}=2\) and \(\{Q_2,P_2\}=1/2\), so neither conjugate pair has the required bracket. A volume-preserving histogram test alone would miss this failure.

Identify brackets of observables and what they measure

Define \(\{A,B\}=(\nabla A)^T J\nabla B=\sum_i(A_{q_i}B_{p_i}-A_{p_i}B_{q_i})\). For any explicitly time-dependent observable \(A(z,t)\), differentiation along Hamiltonian trajectories gives

\[\dot A=\{A,H\}+\partial_t A.\]

The bracket is antisymmetric and obeys the product rule. Computing it with the chain rule in the new variables proves that canonical transformations preserve brackets, even between nonlinear observables. Conversely the fundamental brackets \(\{Q_i,P_j\}=\delta_{ij}\), \(\{Q_i,Q_j\}=\{P_i,P_j\}=0\) are precisely the entries of \(SJS^T=J\).

For a particle set \(L_z=xp_y-yp_x\). Its Hamiltonian flow as a generator sends \(x\) to \(x-\varepsilon y\), \(y\) to \(y+\varepsilon x\) and rotates momenta by the same infinitesimal angle. The angular momentum components satisfy \(\{L_x,L_y\}=L_z\) with cyclic permutations. Derive one entry directly for \(L_x=yp_z-zp_y\) and \(L_y=zp_x-xp_z\). The \(z,p_z\) pair contributes \(xp_y-yp_x\), with the other terms zero. Rotations about two different axes generally fail to commute. The resulting commutator has the form of a rotation about the third axis even though each underlying infinitesimal motion preserves every canonical bracket.

Jacobi's identity \(\{A,\{B,C\}\}+\{B,\{C,A\}\}+\{C,\{A,B\}\}=0\) implies that the bracket of two time-independent conserved quantities is conserved under the same Hamiltonian. If the Hamiltonian is invariant under rotations about \(x\) and \(y\), its invariance about \(z\) follows from this relation and \(\{L_x,L_y\}=L_z\). Conservation alone need not make two observables commute. A central force can conserve all three angular momentum components, but their mutual brackets remain nonzero. For independent commuting integrals in an integrability argument one must check the involution condition explicitly.

Construct a canonical map for any smooth point transformation

Suppose new coordinates depend only on old coordinates, \(Q=f(q)\). Preserve the canonical one-form exactly,

\[p^T dq=P^T dQ=P^T Df\,dq.\]

Thus \(p=(Df)^TP\) and \(P=(Df)^{-T}p\). Applying the same coordinate matrix to momenta works only for a restricted case such as an orthogonal change of coordinates. An extension of position must be accompanied by a reciprocal rescaling of its conjugate momentum. For \(Q=q^2\) on \(q>0\), for example, \(P=p/(2q)\). The free Hamiltonian becomes \(2QP^2/m\); even a physically free particle can acquire a coordinate-dependent expression for its kinetic energy.

Polar coordinates demonstrate why a momentum variable need not equal a measured tangential component. In the plane \(x=r\cos\varphi,y=r\sin\varphi\). Substituting into \(p_xdx+p_ydy\) gives

\[p_r=\mathbf p\cdot\hat r,\]
\[p_\varphi=r\,\mathbf p\cdot\hat\varphi=L_z.\]

The physical tangential momentum is \(p_\varphi/r\). At radius \(0.40\,{\rm m}\) and transverse momentum \(0.60\,{\rm kg\,m/s}\), the canonical angular momentum is \(0.24\,{\rm kg\,m^2/s}\). Treating the transverse component itself as conjugate to angle would make \(\{\varphi,p_\varphi^{\rm wrong}\}=1/r\). The coordinate singularity at \(r=0\) also prevents these canonical polar variables from covering the entire original phase space with one chart.

Generate shears and test a numerical map

A type-two generating function \(F_2(q,P,t)\) defines \(p=\partial_qF_2\), \(Q=\partial_P F_2\), and \(K=H+\partial_tF_2\) where the transformation can be locally inverted. The differential \(d(F_2-QP)\) equals \(p\,dq-P\,dQ+(K-H)\,dt\). Thus the actions differ only by an endpoint term; taking another exterior derivative at fixed time gives the symplectic form condition. For \(F_2=qP+sP^2/(2m)\), the map is \(p=P,Q=q+sp/m\), free drift over time \(s\). For \(F_2=qP+sV(q)\), the map is \(P=p-sV'(q),Q=q\), a potential kick. Their composition is canonical for any step size even though an alternating drift and kick need not give the exact trajectory.

Take a unit-mass, unit-frequency oscillator with \(h=0.5\). A kick followed by drift gives \(P=p-hq\), \(Q=q+hP=(1-h^2)q+hp\). Its amplification matrix has determinant one and trace \(2-h^2=1.75\), so its eigenvalues form a unit-modulus conjugate pair. The numerical phase per step is \(\arccos(1-h^2/2)=0.50536\), slightly ahead of the exact phase \(0.50000\). Starting at \(q=1,p=0\), the first step gives \(Q=0.75,P=-0.50\), and physical energy becomes \(0.40625\) instead of \(0.5\). Exact bracket preservation does not imply exact conservation of the original energy at every point. If \(h>2\) the same matrix remains symplectic but has a real expanding and contracting eigenvalue; geometric preservation is compatible with numerical instability.

For a numerical implementation with analytic equations unavailable, approximate the Jacobian of a one-step map at selected states by perturbing one canonical component at a time. Compute \(S^TJS-J\) after nondimensionalising coordinates and momenta in conjugate pairs. Repeat with smaller perturbations and at different integration tolerances. A determinant close to one in four dimensions is insufficient. Testing the transported brackets of one position–momentum pair and one cross-pair can reveal a swapped column or misplaced mass factor before a long simulation exhibits visible energy drift.

Track canonical area for a transported experimental ensemble

A narrow group of trajectories lets an experimenter estimate a tangent map without applying a separate perturbation to each coordinate at the same time. Prepare a small covariance ellipse in one canonical pair \(z=(q,p)\), propagate each member through a known linear Hamiltonian device, and compute \(\Sigma_{\rm out}=S\Sigma_{\rm in}S^T\). The ellipse area is proportional to \(\sqrt{\det\Sigma}\), and a symplectic map in one pair preserves that area. Its projections can grow or contract individually: a focusing device can reduce position spread while increasing momentum spread or correlation. Measuring only the two marginal widths ignores their covariance and can falsely suggest area growth.

For a free unit-mass drift of duration two in scaled units,

\[S=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&1\end{pmatrix}.\]

Start with \(\Sigma=\mathrm{diag}(1,1)\). The outgoing covariance has entries \(\Sigma_{qq}=5,\Sigma_{pp}=1,\Sigma_{qp}=2\). The apparent rectangle of marginal standard deviations grows by \(\sqrt5\), yet the determinant remains \(5-4=1\). A plot that suppresses the correlation has discarded exactly the information needed for the area test. Noise in the two channels also contributes to observed covariance, so calibrate sensor variances independently before attributing a small apparent growth to physical dissipation or numerical smoothing.

For two pairs it is possible for a perfectly canonical coupling to exchange projected emittances. Take a uniform rotation of the two positions and the same rotation of their conjugate momenta. It is canonical by construction. If the two incoming pairs have different covariance areas, a ninety-degree rotation swaps them completely; at forty-five degrees each outgoing projected pair contains a mixture. The full four-dimensional symplectic structure remains intact. An experimentally available two-dimensional marginal then tests only part of the dynamics. Counting phase-space points in a volume and measuring cross-pair brackets answer different questions about a strongly coupled system.

The eigenvalues of \(J\Sigma\) are invariant under the linear canonical covariance transport. Indeed \(J S=S^{-T}J\) for symplectic \(S\), so \(J\Sigma_{\rm out}=S^{-T}(J\Sigma_{\rm in})S^T\), a similarity transformation. For a positive covariance these eigenvalues occur as conjugate imaginary pairs, and their positive magnitudes give symplectic covariance scales. This algebraic test can distinguish loss of correlations in a fitted transfer matrix from actual exchange between pairs. It assumes linear propagation of the ensemble. A nonlinear canonical map can stretch a finite ellipse into a curved shape whose covariance determinant changes while its exact enclosed area remains preserved.

Use generating functions to find a moving normal-mode basis

Consider a two-dimensional isotropic oscillator and rotate its coordinate axes by a prescribed time-dependent angle \(\alpha(t)\). Let \(Q=R(\alpha)^Tq\), \(P=R(\alpha)^Tp\) for a proper planar rotation. At any fixed time the map is orthogonal and symplectic. To derive the Hamiltonian in these moving variables, use \(F_2(q,P,t)=q^TR(\alpha)P\). Its time derivative at fixed old \(q\) and new \(P\) is \(-\dot\alpha(Q_xP_y-Q_yP_x)\), with the sign corresponding to axes rotating counterclockwise in the laboratory. The transformed Hamiltonian is therefore

\[K=H_0(Q,P)-\dot\alpha L_z.\]

For constant rotation rate and an isotropic potential the new Hamiltonian has no explicit time dependence. Its value is the combination energy minus rotation rate times angular momentum encountered in the Noether argument. Hamilton's equations now include \(-\dot\alpha\,\hat z\times Q\) in \(\dot Q\) relative to the rotated physical velocity. Thus the transformed canonical momentum \(P\) equals the rotated laboratory momentum rather than \(m\dot Q\). Confusing the two yields incorrect initial conditions for a rotating-frame simulation even if the transformation matrix at the starting instant is the identity.

Take \(m=1.0\,{\rm kg}\), a laboratory point initially at \(q=(0.20,0)\,{\rm m}\) with \(p=(0,0.30)\,{\rm kg\,m/s}\), and axes initially aligned but rotating at \(2.0\,{\rm rad/s}\). The canonical \(P_y\) is \(0.30\,{\rm kg\,m/s}\). The derivative of the rotating-frame coordinate is

\[0.30-2.0(0.20)=-0.10\,{\rm m/s}.\]

It moves in the negative rotating-frame \(y\) direction while its laboratory velocity points positive \(y\). The angular momentum is \(0.060\,{\rm kg\,m^2/s}\), so \(K\) lies \(0.120\,{\rm J}\) below the laboratory Hamiltonian. Both position–momentum transformations preserve brackets; the extra term derives from the moving basis, not a failure of canonical geometry.

An oscillator with two nearly equal natural frequencies provides another reason to track the generating function. If one diagonalises a slowly changing stiffness matrix at each time and keeps only the instantaneous diagonal frequencies, the omitted rotation of the eigenvectors couples the nominal modes through a term of this type. When the eigendirections turn rapidly near a degeneracy, the coupling can be appreciable even if both eigenvalues change little. Measuring the orientation as well as the eigenfrequencies allows the size of this effect to be estimated before fitting amplitude transfer to a spurious dissipation term.

Compare exact phase transport with a dissipative fit

A damped oscillator with \(m\ddot q+c\dot q+kq=0\) has equations \(\dot q=p/m\), \(\dot p=-kq-cp/m\). In the physical \((q,p)\) plane their divergence is \(-c/m\). A small material phase-space area therefore contracts as \(A(t)=A(0)e^{-ct/m}\). No time-independent canonical coordinate change on the same two-dimensional isolated phase space can turn that physical contraction into a Hamiltonian flow preserving area. This conclusion concerns the reduced dissipative description. An expanded system including the degrees of freedom that absorb energy can still be Hamiltonian.

For \(c/m=0.40\,{\rm s^{-1}}\), a pair of independent initial perturbations spanning area one in calibrated coordinates spans only \(0.449\) after two seconds. A numerical step of \(0.10\,{\rm s}\) fitted to that exact map should have determinant \(e^{-0.04}=0.9608\). Forcing its determinant to one during system identification would remove a real dissipative measurement. Conversely a conservative oscillator propagated by forward Euler at matched units and \(h=0.10\) has amplification determinant \(1+h^2=1.01\), corresponding to a spurious area growth. A geometric diagnostic is interpretable only after deciding what physical system and coordinate pair were actually observed.

The pair \(q,v\) differs from canonical \(q,p\) by a constant mass scaling for an ordinary single particle.
